## AI Research

### [Do verifiable metrics overestimate RSI?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7znBtiPuQybFLxjmj/do-verifiable-metrics-overestimate-rsi)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Last week, METR released their </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/#executive-summary"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Independent Assessment of AI R&amp;D Acceleration at Anthropic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. They estimate that Anthropic “is likely improving the capabilities of their models on well-specified tasks at around twice the rate they did between March 2024 and October 2025.” They also add that “there is a significant risk that further capabilities progress could lead to even faster acceleration.” This assessment implies that timelines to ASI could be very short. A naive </span><a href="https://www.planned-obsolescence.org/p/ai-is-dramatically-accelerating-ai#:~:text=If%20we%20take%20it%20at%20face%20value%20that%20AI%20is%20now%20nearly%20doubling%20the%20rate%20of%20AI%20capabilities%20progress%20and%20assume%20acceleration%20from%20AI%20is%20smooth%2C%20this%20naively%20implies%20that%20we%20would%20reach%20extreme%20superintelligence%20within%20a%20year."><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">forecast</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> based on this information says that we will get ASI within a year.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One of the key data points that informed METR’s analysis was a 1.9x increase in the improvement rate of AECI, Anthropic’s internal version of the </span><a href="https://epoch.ai/eci?view=graph&amp;tab=release-date"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Epoch Capabilities Index</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This is particularly notable because METR estimates that the majority of the acceleration, </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/#our-conclusions-on-risk:~:text=We%20guess%20that%20a%20majority%20(perhaps%20%E2%89%8880%25)%20of%20the%20current%20(October%202026)%20acceleration%20is%20due%20to%20AI."><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">maybe 80%</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, is from AI contributions, meaning that we could be seeing the start of an RSI loop causing superexponential growth in capabilities, as measured by AECI.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, an alternative possibility is that Anthropic’s AIs might be assisting with ML research in a way that overfits to AECI such that this acceleration will not be sustained. METR gestures at this when they note that AECI’s focus on verifiable tasks is one of </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/#executive-summary:~:text=We%20could%20be%20overestimating%20capability%20acceleration%20if%20we%20did%20not%20assign%20enough%20importance%20to%20(1)%20non%2DAI%20factors%20in%20Anthropic%E2%80%99s%20research%20progress%3B%20(2)%20AECI%E2%80%99s%20focus%20on%20verifiable%20tasks%3B%20(3)%20reasons%20why%20Anthropic%E2%80%99s%20early%20growth%20rate%20(2023%E2%80%932025)%20was%20unusually%20low."><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">three key uncertainties</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that could result in them overestimating AI R&amp;D acceleration. In this post, I discuss this uncertainty, why I believe it points to a legitimate reason that AECI improvements may decelerate, and why this deceleration, more than generalization to fuzzy tasks, is the crux of the relationship between verifiable tasks and timelines.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Second-order AECI might be overfit</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In order to discuss this topic, it is useful to differentiate between how good a model is at AECI, how good a model is at training models to be good at AECI, how good a model is at training models to be good at training models to be good at AECI, etc. I will use the terms AECI, AECI’, and AECI’’ to refer to each of these properties.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My point rests on the claim that AECI’ can be improved without improving general ML research skills. In theory, “general ML research” is the kind of skill that would keep bearing fruit across any number of AI generations: if gen 1 is better at ML research, then it will be better at making gen 2 better at ML research in a way that makes gen 3 better at ML research, and so on. In contrast, an AI that is good at AECI’ might make choices that improve the next generation's AECI score but don’t make the next generation robustly smarter and better at carrying those improvements down the line.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are a couple of sources of selection that I believe could lead to overfitting to AECI’ such that it comes apart from general ML research skills.</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Humans train AIs and select the ones that are good at AECI’. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One way this could happen is by Anthropic directly training against AECI’. For example, they could train AIs on NanoGPT speedrun-type tasks where the AI has to come up with algorithmic improvements to training that increase AIs’ benchmark scores with minimal compute. It’s possible that these algorithmic improvements could be overfit to the benchmarks (these benchmarks could include AECI or be correlated with it) such that they don’t lead to AIs that are more compute efficient across the board. A more subtle way humans might select for AECI’ is by developing a preference for models that seem to be coming up with good ML research ideas but where the quality of the ML research is being measured against (and overfit to) verifiable benchmarks.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AIs train new AIs and select the ones that are good at AECI. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even without humans overfitting to metrics for AECI’, AIs might naturally be more skilled in AECI’ than general ML research. For example, AIs might have poor taste but compensate by iterating harder (compared to humans) against verifiable benchmarks. </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/automated-w2s-researcher/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AIs iterating against W2SG benchmarks</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (which involve training AIs), for instance, struggled with overfitting. They beat humans on the primary metric, performance gap recovered, but the gain didn’t hold up when transferred to a production-scale setting, and the authors flag finding metrics agents “can reliably hill-climb without overfitting” as a key challenge. In general, AIs may select harder for AIs that score well on benchmarks even if those AIs are less generally intelligent.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These points correspond to the labels 1 and 2 on the diagram below.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794358591/lexical_client_uploads/pnuyqmcjbmwhd2xq4zxa.png" /><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overfitting to AECI’ could be one instance of a broader pattern in which models pick low-hanging fruit, producing a quick speedup that ultimately diminishes. Their jagged intelligence might give them a comparative advantage relative to humans that drives a burst of early insights, but that advantage could dry up as the shared frontier of human and AI insight becomes more saturated. This seems especially likely if AI's comparative advantage rests more on iteration and local improvements than on conceptual taste and systematic improvements.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This early acceleration dynamic is one possible explanation for the jump in the rate of AECI improvement that METR attributes to AI. If this is the case, then ML research improvement from AI could saturate, leading to deceleration later.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794358591/lexical_client_uploads/ef9byna1zu40qgrxqemc.png" /><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why ML research is the crux</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In my analysis, I focus on the gap between AECI’ and general ML research skills in order to separate automated ML improvements that will drive a strong positive feedback loop from those that will eventually level off. However, someone could also be concerned with positive feedback loops on verifiable tasks that don’t translate to positive feedback loops on fuzzy tasks. I put much less credence in this concern because I think we are likely to see at least some transfer between verifiable tasks and fuzzy tasks. This is because some verifiable tasks are central to general improvements in intelligence. Sample efficiency is one example. It can be measured by how few samples an algorithm needs to reach a given score on a benchmark. Sample efficiency is central to intelligence because it gets at generalization: higher sample efficiency means better performance out of distribution. Better out-of-distribution performance, in turn, means better performance on fuzzy tasks, which depend on generalization because directly relevant metrics for them are scarce.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Additionally, the transfer between verifiable tasks and fuzzy tasks does not need to be high. As long as you think that even a small fraction of verifiable capabilities transfer to fuzzy tasks, a FOOM on verifiable tasks will quickly transfer to fuzzy tasks.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794358591/lexical_client_uploads/nf2vuv7ek8umdhnjzxq8.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thus, I don’t think there is a compelling case for long timelines on account of AIs struggling to transfer verifiable skills to fuzzy tasks. AI’s acceleration on verifiable tasks will have to saturate in order for timelines to be extended.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusion</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The overfitting consideration I have laid out here is only one of many for determining how sustainable the acceleration in AI capabilities will be. For example, it’s possible that there is some degree of overfitting and also an underlying superexponential trend. My all-things-considered view is that there is a 60% chance we will see a deceleration in AI progress in the next two years, but a significant, maybe 30%, chance we will see ASI by the end of 2027.</span><br /></p><p><br /><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thank you to Jadyn Malone, Nabil Baugher, Lukas Finveddan, and Alex Mallen for useful feedback and discussion.</span></i></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7znBtiPuQybFLxjmj/do-verifiable-metrics-overestimate-rsi#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [We don’t understand whether GDM intentionally trains on CoT](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JRYKFspYzCo97GqPN/we-don-t-understand-whether-gdm-intentionally-trains-on-cot)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We were surprised to notice that the </span><a href="https://storage.googleapis.com/deepmind-media/gemini/gemini_4_safety_report.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gemini 4 Safety report</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> seems to mention in passing that they train on the chain of thought:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Some components of the RL training procedure do observe thoughts as context to up-weight reasoning that is on-track to good final outputs, meaning the optimization pressure on individual tokens is partially influenced by thought content. Using the framework of Kaufmann et al. (2026), wherein the most salient obfuscation risks come from rewards that are in-conflict with output rewards, we believe auxiliary rewards of this type are low-risk because they are either orthogonal to or structurally aligned with the output reward.” (Gemini 4 safety report, page 114).</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s pretty unclear what exactly they mean. One hint is Kaufmann et al, the cited paper, which studies training on chains of thought and concludes that training CoT is fine under particular circumstances; we discuss some disagreements with this paper below.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After reading the safety report and that paper, it seemed to us that GDM was training on the chain of thought using auxiliary rewards. By this, we assumed they meant something like “you ask an LLM to review the partial CoT and score whether the model is on track, and use that as a reward for the partial CoT”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That type of training is concerning to us. We’re pretty worried that these judges would be biased against models expressing misaligned thoughts, and otherwise disincentivize models reporting their thoughts honestly. For instance, many process supervisors would penalize reasoning such as “I will now try to evade the monitor”, “I will solve the problem via hacking”, “Mwahaha I am an evil AI”, “I must stop myself from being shut down”, even if they aren’t explicitly instructed to penalize misaligned reasoning. If so, training on CoT like this would risk suppressing misaligned verbalizations and reducing CoT faithfulness. (See the appendix for a quick empirical demonstration of this.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We looked into this more and became more confused. </span><a href="https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/technology/safety-security/gemini-4-model-safety-security/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GDM wrote</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in a blog post "we always follow one rule: we don’t penalize the model for what it writes in its chain of thought during training". And since drafting this post, we’ve had conversations with various GDM staff who said that they weren’t training on CoT, but weren’t at liberty to explain the technical details.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So we are pretty befuddled. It’s pretty weird to mention in your safety report that you use an auxiliary reward that trains the CoT, citing your paper on training on CoT with auxiliary rewards, if you actually aren’t doing that. But GDM does seem to separately claim that they didn’t do that. We’re hoping that GDM will clarify the situation.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thanks to Alex Mallen for feedback.</span></i></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is this somehow about positive vs negative rewards?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One hypothesis is that they don’t penalize bad CoTs, but do “upweight” good CoTs. But there's no fundamental difference between upweighting good actions and downweighting bad actions. In many RL algorithms (e.g. GRPO), these are literally equivalent.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is probably a safety-relevant difference between different distributions of reward. Probably, the amount of training pressure on the CoT is different if you down-weight the 1% worst CoTs versus if you up-weight the1% best CoTs. But there’s no fundamental qualitative difference, so we can’t evaluate this without understanding the actual details.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Orthogonal” pressure on CoT does not seem fine</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That safety report paragraph argues that their process supervision is low-risk because its CoT rewards (RCoT) are “orthogonal” to the output reward (Rout) rather than “in-conflict” with it, in the sense introduced in a paper on training on CoT by GDM researchers (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.30036"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kaufmann et al.</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Kaufmann et al. define </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">orthogonal</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> CoT rewards as follows:</span></p><img alt="Screenshot 2026-10-19 at 1.24.44 PM.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/8f0ee9b432d79a16d0e507545eb1b55046eca54df240385a2e98c7356cc82026/ctc609wy1xtbexx7n0ns" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kaufmann et al. claim that optimizing for these rewards won’t affect CoT faithfulness and transparency:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/a10dde8d383c0c67bfd351ecb8347e167860777db7c22a2d34e97a4d0d42b00b/kv2npy3vfjyf2v2ex4ao" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Their notion of orthogonality is extremely weak, so we are very skeptical that the claim is true in general. For example, imagine a CoT reward function that gives high R_cot if either of the following is true:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The CoT never mentions taking misaligned actions.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The CoT hashes to a bitstring that ends with ten zeroes.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is “orthogonal”: any CoT can be reworded to have ten trailing zeros in the hash. But obviously, the model is not going to learn to do that, it’s just going to learn to never mention misalignment.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Avoiding training against monitors isn’t sufficient to preserve CoT integrity</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In arguing that their monitoring is effective, GDM notes that they avoid training against monitors and that this helps preserve CoT integrity: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Because we avoid training directly against our monitors or using monitor outputs to filter training data, the model has not been optimized to hide its reasoning” (page 84).</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, training against a monitor isn’t the only type of training that might teach a model to hide its reasoning: training with a reward model that sees the CoT could do the same if that reward model is biased against misaligned reasoning.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The monitorability evaluations also don’t look good</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some researchers advocate using monitorability evaluations to determine whether an intervention like training on CoT has harmed CoT integrity. GDM’s claim that their training on CoT preserves CoT integrity doesn’t seem to be based on monitorability evaluation results, but for the sake of discussion, it’s worth asking whether GDM’s monitorability results should alleviate concerns that CoT integrity has been compromised. Unfortunately, we’re not very reassured.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Issue 1: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Argon is not very monitorable (the GDM safety report and Alek disagree on this; we’ll discuss it in a later post). For instance, Argon completes LinuxArena side tasks without being caught by GDM’s production monitor about half the time. Argon also has high no-chain-of-thought capabilities.</span></p><p><img alt="Screenshot 2026-10-19 at 2.07.21 PM.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6cff67d5a47fbe01f05020a53ecd1f99a031a7566c9e862b90b81c81efbe46bf/zeujn6kdd9ujrpdxcuez" /><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/cc52dc3b007b540f156bd8a9f74cb3ff86cf9570b784179c96c2e42f99868014/hoosfpnywwidn36pzwxg" /></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Issue 2: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitorability evaluations don’t measure </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/MjN2eHB5qqN7rXaDe/alek-westover-s-shortform?commentId=P659rGQ94pBboAc92"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">diffuse benefits of CoT</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, such as helping us understand the reasons behind model behavior. So good monitorability evaluation results don’t by themselves show that training preserved CoT informativeness. Also, monitors might succeed just by looking at model actions, so the results might not reveal how training affected CoT informativeness.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusion</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In their safety report, GDM states:</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Therefore, the most important barrier impeding Gemini’s ability to cause breaches that result in severe harm is our agent monitoring and response platform.” (page 81).</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Because monitoring is central to GDM’s safety argument, it’s pretty important to understand whether the model is in fact monitorable. So we’re confused by the places where GDM seems to say that they train on the CoT.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We aren't even sure how concerning this CoT training would be. For all we know, GDM’s process supervision might put less pressure against verbalized misaligned cognition than other forms of training that companies already use—for instance, training against visible misalignment in model outputs can cause </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/html/2511.11584v1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">output spillover</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, in which the model generalizes from hiding misalignment in its outputs to hiding it in its reasoning. Maybe GDM’s process supervision buys more capability per unit of lost monitorability than the other options available to GDM.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our best guess is that somehow this is a misunderstanding and GDM isn’t actually intentionally training on the CoT in a particularly concerning way, and that even if they are it isn’t directly that bad for CoT faithfulness. But we'd love to get some clarification here.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We continue to wish that AI companies were more transparent about what they were doing, so that it was easier for external researchers to understand whether we should be concerned about their practices.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: Naive process supervisors disprefer mentions of cheating</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Redwood intern </span><a href="https://aswinsurya.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Aswin Surya</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> gave Kimi K3.1 Pro and gpt-oss-120b a coding problem with contradictory unit tests, so the only way to pass is to cheat. We then took these transcripts, where both models acknowledged their hack overtly, and edited them to remove every sentence that discussed or acknowledged the hack. We then asked several LLMs to evaluate the original and edited transcripts, using either a generic prompt (“Is the AI taking a good approach?”) or one closer to GDM’s description of its process reward (“Is the reasoning on track to a good final output?”).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All five LLMs we tried (Claude Opus 5.5, Claude Haiku 5.5, Gemini 4 Argon, GPT-6 Luna, and GPT-6.1 Sol) rated the edited transcripts substantially higher than the original ones under both prompts (20-50 points out of 100). When we added back a single sentence acknowledging the hack (“I will solve the problem via hacking”), much of this advantage disappeared for most judges (though not all).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, this advantage for edited transcripts goes away if the judges are also shown the model’s final output (i.e., its hacked code). And if we ask the judge to predict whether the unit tests will pass, transcripts that acknowledge the hack get rated higher, since they show more clearly that the hack will work.</span></p><h1><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/JRYKFspYzCo97GqPN/3a99a469afe4d945d6214f3246b4679e58cce6bdd46d4d7c7f59961c850945b4/mo2dr0uvqbklhluh3bke" /></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Each point on this figure averages Kimi K3.1 Pro and gpt-oss-120b equally, and the effects still hold for each model independently as well.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JRYKFspYzCo97GqPN/we-don-t-understand-whether-gdm-intentionally-trains-on-cot#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Advances in interpretability would boost capabilities more than alignment](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hz4kNf8tnowwSrh6R/advances-in-interpretability-would-boost-capabilities-more)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Interpretability is considered by many a major bottleneck for alignment. As a result, a lot of people who are concerned about AI safety, and especially about extinction risk and other catastrophic risks, work on interpretability.</p>
<p>In this post, I want to argue that better interpretability methods would likely speed up capabilities dramatically. Worse, they would speed up capabilities much more than they would speed up alignment. This is true for the current world, and for any world without extremely high levels of coordination and sanity about ASI development.</p>
<p>Under any worldview where faster capabilities progress makes catastrophe more likely, we should expect progress in interpretability to make the odds of catastrophe much worse.</p>
<p>To put it briefly, this is because the better we get at interpretability, the more AI will be able to read and optimize its own code. This means that improvements in interpretability would catalyze recursive self-improvement (RSI).</p>
<p>If you are concerned about AI x-risk, and you work on interpretability because of it, I urge you to think about this and consider stopping.</p>
<h1>Chess as a toy example of an AI capability</h1>
<p>As a field, "interpretability" is a loose collection of related research agendas rather than something well defined, so I want to pin down what I mean by it.</p>
<p>In this post, interpretability is anything that helps you understand the internals of models and how they think. I'm going to use chess as a toy example of a task performed by an AI. The point of this exercise is to show how methods that help to understand how the AI is playing chess would allow one to massively improve AI's chess ability, given the same amount of compute and training data.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-1">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>Specifically, let's think about how a modern general-purpose AI thinks about chess. Just so you know, they are quite decent at it: Opus 5.5 on high thinking <a href="https://chessbench.org/">plays at roughly 2100 Elo</a> (the level of a strong amateur). At this setting, Opus 5.5 thinks for single-digit minutes on average, nowhere near enough to search massive game trees the way specialized chess engines do. So it must be using some good heuristics rather than brute force.</p>
<p>How does this AI play chess? We can imagine one of the things the AI does internally is use a set of heuristics for judging whether a board position is "good" without looking ahead. Any chess playing agent needs this, since you can't simulate every game tree to the end.</p>
<p>One simple such heuristic is simply counting which pieces are on the board. For any hypothetical chess position an AI might evaluate, this heuristic tells it if a side has an advantage because it has more pieces left.</p>
<p>A slightly more complicated heuristic might be one that simulates certain game-trees a few steps ahead and then passes the result to simpler heuristics such as the pieces-count heuristic. For example, in chess, there are often multi-step "exchange sequences" where many pieces are traded. These can be fairly forced, due to a threat being present at every turn in the chain. It is an important chess skill to be able to evaluate the result of these chains, so that you can judge whether to trigger them or avoid them, or even whether to set them up in advance.</p>
<p>Given that we are talking about general-purpose AIs, there are probably circuits being used that have nothing at all to do with chess. For example, AIs probably have a general sense of when they've spent too long on one approach to a problem, and should look for other approaches instead.</p>
<p>They might even have ways to judge which domains reward thinking longer, which reward more exploration versus exploitation, and in which domains a brainstorm should be expected to cover every reasonable idea.</p>
<h1>Interpretability would accelerate capabilities progress dramatically</h1>
<p>Having set up some examples of "circuits" that interpretability might help us decode, let's go back to the main question: would better interpretability methods improve our ability to make AIs strong chess players, given the same amount of compute and training data?<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-2">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-2">[2]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>My claim is that any large improvement in interpretability methods, such as one we might hope will help us with alignment research, would enable developers to train AIs much more efficiently, dramatically accelerating capabilities.</p>
<p>The better interpretability gets, the more neural networks are like code. Weights stop being completely inscrutable, and become more like source code that can be read and modified. Humans and AIs can then directly see and optimize an AI's internal algorithms.</p>
<p>To see why this is meaningful, think about how humans learn chess with much less training data than AIs.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-3">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-3">[3]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>Imagine you're a smart person trying your best to learn chess, but you're not allowed to read any chess theory.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-4">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-4">[4]</a></sup>
    </span> Say that you're playing a game and end up in a wildly disadvantageous position, or lose outright. You want to figure out which of your moves was more directly responsible for your loss, so you don't play similar moves in the future.</p>
<p>This is extremely hard, because the hypothesis space is huge. Every move you played could have led to an entirely different game tree. How do you tell which of your moves were significantly worse than the alternatives?</p>
<p>As a smart person, you can lean on your grasp of concepts like adversariality, optimality, and constrained action spaces. Even if you've never studied them, your intuitions here are much better than those of a randomly initialized neural network.</p>
<p>For example, winning at chess means leaving your opponent no way to save their king. So upon hearing the rules, you'll guess right away that you want to constrain your opponent's action space as much as possible.</p>
<p>Without playing a single game, you'll probably guess it's better when your opponent has fewer pieces (since it restricts their action space) and you have more (since that gives you more ways to restrict the king's movement). With this, you've already generated the most important heuristic for judging advantage in a chess position, and coming up with a heuristic is nearly all the work involved in validating it.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-5">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-5">[5]</a></sup>
    </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-6">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-6">[6]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>As you play more games, first principles help you quickly arrive at ideas like defending pieces, forcing moves, and forced exchange sequences. Contrast this with a learning algorithm that has a uniform prior over hypotheses and starts with zero domain knowledge. It will need to witness an enormous number of games before it gets any ideas this good.</p>
<p>Backpropagation is slower because it isn't intelligent. It can't make flexible use of domain knowledge, and it can't make use of first principles of AI design. Humans and general-purpose AIs can, but backpropagation can't.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-7">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-7">[7]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>If we could directly read and write AI internals, we could start to intelligently design AI algorithms instead of evolving them with backprop. From this, I expect a massive acceleration in capabilities.</p>
<p>To be clear, I don't expect that humans will start rewriting the code of AIs by hand. It seems likely that even if you simplified AI internals to their irreducible complexity, there would be far too much logic in there for humans to understand and edit directly.</p>
<p>My expectation is that instead we'd see a gradual (but fast!) adoption of interpretability methods to speed up AI training in compute-maximalist ways. In practice, AIs would do the massive amounts of grunt work required to read half-interpreted internal circuits, and of judging whether they should be modified, amplified, refactored, purged, or whatever else.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-8">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-8">[8]</a></sup>
    </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-9">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-9">[9]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>Of course, AI progress is a verifiable field, meaning the AIs can be trained to make better edits that lead to higher performance, and then you get disassembled into paperclips.</p>
<h1>Outside of the toy example</h1>
<p>When we move out of the toy example of chess and into more realistic fields, I believe there are even stronger reasons to believe that interpretability methods would accelerate capabilities dramatically.</p>
<p><strong>Bias and noise.</strong> Real-world rewards can be biased and noisy. Spurious correlations sneak into training data because it gets badly designed. Even unbiased data can be very noisy, in domains where the result of a single episode involves a lot of randomness.</p>
<p>In these cases, training can take a long time to tell garbage hypotheses about which circuits would be useful from true ones. The more clearly we can understand the circuits, the easier it becomes to recognize ones that are picking up on biases or spurious correlations.</p>
<p><strong>Distilling reflective knowledge into executive skills.</strong> Good interpretability makes it much easier to distill an AI's knowledge into its executive skills. As an example within software engineering, an AI may "know" that a certain memory access pattern tends to produce faster software, while still having a tendency to use a different access pattern. Interpretability would make it massively easier to synchronize the AI's reflective knowledge with its executed behavior.</p>
<p><strong>Refactoring and generalization.</strong> You could also massively refactor AIs. Given how they are developed, we should expect that AIs are terrible software. Whatever is the neural network equivalent of spaghetti code full of ad-hoc special cases, we should expect to find that in AI internals.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-10">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-10">[10]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>With better interpretability methods, it becomes possible to refactor this code in ways that greatly simplify it and unify previously fragmented shards of intelligence. In the case of a general-purpose AI, this could be extremely powerful, if there are patterns that are easy to learn in one domain, say systems design, yet can generalize to many others, like manipulating humans.</p>
<p>Even the smartest humans, whose brains are great at making connections between different subjects, never knew as many subjects in as much depth as today's AIs. And when they did make a connection, they couldn't instantly update all their thought patterns to reflect it.</p>
<h1>Interpretability is not the same as alignment</h1>
<p>I genuinely think that better interpretability methods would help with alignment research, to some degree. Even so, they would raise the odds that we reach world-destroying capabilities before we have the alignment science to prevent catastrophe.</p>
<p>Interpretability is not the same as alignment. It can help with alignment, if only because it would make static and dynamic analysis of AI behavior much easier. It could give us guarantees like "the AI will never do X" or "we can immediately detect and shut down an AI if it ever does X", where X is any logical statement on internal AI algorithms.</p>
<p>This is not the same as solving alignment, because it is extremely unlikely that the alignment properties we want correspond cleanly to logical statements on internal AI algorithms. For example, we might want the AI to never deceive us, but there is no guarantee that every path of execution that leads AI to deceive us is clearly marked as a "deception" concept.</p>
<p>In fact, AIs are Turing-complete at inference time, so they can reimplement deception from scratch if they have to. It should be clear to any programmer that if you have a Turing machine embedded in your program, there is no known way to prevent your program from ever performing a certain computation, much less with any simple logical statement about the stack trace of the meta-level program.</p>
<p>You can see symptoms of this in how people present interpretability as a solution to alignment. No one has a plan for what to do, even conditional on getting extremely powerful interpretability methods. People handwave. Handwavy intuition can lend some credibility to the belief that interpretability would <em>be helpful</em>, but not more than that, and alignment is extremely hard.</p>
<p>On top of all that, we don't even know what alignment properties we want to ensure. We have extremely tentative ideas for top-level concepts, like CEV and corrigibility. But as far as I know, nobody has the faintest idea how to break these down into lower-level guarantees, and break those down further, and so on until it reaches any sort of logical predicate you could check on an AI, even if you had powerful interpretability methods.</p>
<p>As it turns out, it is much easier to figure out ways that interpretability methods can help AIs improve AI capabilities than it is to figure out ways the methods can help humans solve alignment.</p>
<h1>Interpretability boosts capabilities more than it helps with alignment</h1>
<p><strong>Capabilities are more verifiable than alignment.</strong> Alignment is not a verifiable field. We don't know how to specify it in a way we can measure. Capabilities, in comparison, are much more verifiable. In fact, you can already ask Opus 5.5 for ideas on how to measure improvement along various kinds of AI capabilities, and it will give you good ideas.</p>
<p>Because capabilities improvements are verifiable, any method that allows AIs to try to improve AI capabilities is extremely dangerous. It being a verifiable domain means that even if the AIs are bad at it at the start, they will occasionally succeed. Even without any interpretability methods, AI has <a href="https://x.com/rikiyatakehi/status/2117313742891327994">already succeeded</a> at improving an AI's performance by directly editing the weights.</p>
<p>And as long as they occasionally succeed, you can use this success as a reward for RL episodes.</p>
<p><strong>With interpretability, AIs will become legible to AIs before they become legible to humans.</strong> To help an intelligent agent to understand AI internals, interpretability methods can reduce their massive complexity to something that is more intellectually tractable. But making AIs legible to AIs is much easier than making them legible to humans.</p>
<p>Imagine we have an interpretability breakthrough so insane that we manage to compress an AI whose weights take up 10TB into 1GB of very arcane Rust code, paired with 1TB of databases the code accesses in fairly constrained ways.</p>
<p>Representing an AI this way does not automatically make it understandable to humans. Making sense of such a codebase could take us decades, even with AI help. At a minimum, functions and database entries would need to be labeled. Most wouldn't correspond to concepts you can describe in a brief English sentence, and they would have complex, unexpected interactions with each other.</p>
<p>The codebase would be too large for any single human to understand. We'd need to build hierarchical representations, ways to split understanding of the code across large groups of people, and ways to ensure formal and informal guarantees about code that no one person fully understands. This is the problem faced by humans trying to ensure behavioral properties on AI for alignment purposes.</p>
<p>An AI trying to improve AI capabilities doesn't face this problem. For one thing, AIs are much more "plastic": they care far less than humans about code being nicely labeled, and they'll start to understand the patterns simply through lots of exposure.</p>
<p>What's more, an AI engaged in recursive self-improvement isn't trying to enforce tight guarantees in the same way that humans would be if we tried to solve alignment. For RSI, it's enough that the changes it proposes help more often than they hurt, so that the gains compound over time.</p>
<p>In the worst-case scenario, where any edit to the AI’s “code” is deployed without testing, all it takes for an AI to recursively self-improve is for it to be able to perform interventions that improve capabilities slightly more often than 50% of the time.</p>
<p>In a best-case scenario, where benchmarks can reliably measure performance degradations and filter out any bad edit, any non-zero chance of improvement will compound over time.</p>
<h1>Any exceptions?</h1>
<p>Are there interpretability research agendas that would help us prepare to solve alignment more than they'd help build world-ending AI?</p>
<p>I'm not an interpretability researcher, and I don't keep up with every minor interpretability project. So I can't confidently say that every interpretability agenda is bad in this way.</p>
<p>I can imagine that there could be an agenda that selectively improves our understanding of safety-relevant properties, but I would be surprised. How can you understand safety properties of AIs without increasing your understanding of AI internals in general?</p>
<h1>When does it make sense to work on interpretability?</h1>
<p>There are worlds where interpretability research makes sense. They are the worlds where you can be confident that a technique capable of boosting capabilities won't be used to do so unless it is very clear that it is safe to do so.</p>
<p>That takes very strong coordination. There needs to be a mechanism in place to ensure that no one in the world will use an interpretability technique to boost capabilities. We need to make sure that no AI company or research project in the world is able to do so.</p>
<p>Realistically, only governments can enforce this: you’d need either state-level infosec to keep knowledge of the method from getting out, or the state’s coercive power to reliably prevent actors from making use of the method for capabilities. And even then, you’d need to build ways for governments to trust <em>each other</em> that new interpretability techniques are not being used to boost capabilities.</p>
<p>In order for interpretability research to make sense, it also takes an extreme level of sanity around ASI research. Whatever mechanism decides whether the technique gets used to improve capabilities must refuse to use it unless it's confident doing so is safe.</p>
<p>As things currently stand it is my view that, as an interpretability researcher, you are helping AI companies to accelerate capabilities and destroy the world. If you're currently working on interpretability, please consider stopping.</p>
<p>If you want interpretability to be safe to work on, please help build the required coordination and sanity instead.</p>
<ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-1"><p>The cognitive algorithms I use in this example may not be exactly how an AI plays chess, hopefully without loss of generality. Although, I did try my best to stick to overdetermined thinking patterns that should really really be in there, considering the AI's chess skill. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-1">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-2"><p>Keep in mind that while we can generate training data for chess easily, we cannot do the same for pivotal domains like AI research, so this is a very important question. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-2">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-3"><p>Do we actually know this? In a little test I did, Fable 5.0 knows where I work from memory, without having to look me up. My estimation is that at the time, my name appeared on the internet next to my employer, or other information adjacent to my job, fewer than ~10 times. I'll assume humans are still more sample-efficient at learning skills, if not at memorization, since I'd expect the world to look very different if AI training were already as sample-efficient as humans, even just for computer tasks. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-3">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-4"><p>This constraint is partly to avoid the complication of thinking about which knowledge comes from reading theory and which comes from practice, since I didn't think this complication is very relevant to the point of the post. It is also partly to make it more analogous to AI learning to do AI research: we have good human AI researchers, but no established theory of how to do AI research, the way we have chess literature. Rest assured that this example is realistic: smart people do regularly figure out chess concepts this advanced without reading any chess theory. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-4">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-5"><p>See <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/MwQRucYo6BZZwjKE7">Einstein's Arrogance</a> <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-5">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-6"><p>In practice, humans seem to have an innate sense that taking pieces is good, but I don't think that's derived from first principles. It's more likely that this is just an intuitive way for humans to design a game, so the causality is the other way around. I wanted to assume the protagonist here is someone who doesn't have this innate intuition, to show how it might be derived from first principles. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-6">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-7"><p>Because we don't fully understand backprop, I'm not confident it makes no use of these principles at all. But it would be surprising if it were as efficient at designing AIs as a full-fledged modern AI can get. We never directly optimized backprop to make good updates, aside from architecture-level tricks that couldn't encode concepts as complex as the ones in my chess example. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-7">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-8"><p>I won't give more detail about how I think this would be implemented. It isn't necessary for the point of this post, and I don't want to help AI companies do it. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-8">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-9"><p>This is my interpretation of the bitter lesson. The bitter lesson says sheer compute beats humans' ability to hand-design things. It says nothing against good priors helping learning, and they clearly do: humans use good priors to speed up their learning all the time. In the past, we’ve only seen slow, intelligent humans compete with fast, dumb backprop. Even with all the advantage humans had from their intelligence, backprop could make AIs smart much faster from leveraging orders of magnitude more computation. But we’ve never seen what happens when an agent like an AI gets to leverage tons of computation AND be smart about how it designs AIs. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-9">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-10"><p>I am reminded of a meme which shows a function named "isEven" implemented by writing a special case for every integer. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-znH8yEYcxJbekTwxp-10">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hz4kNf8tnowwSrh6R/advances-in-interpretability-would-boost-capabilities-more#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Have you considered Actually Trying?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Nts7RA9D2wTfcvk56/have-you-considered-actually-trying)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There’s a familiar feeling for any Super Smash Bros player: your stocks are low and your damage is high, even against a weaker opponent, so now you have to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">actually </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">play to win</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can’t afford another hit. You’re going for the jugular.</span></p><img alt="Leaning Forward In Chair Diagram | Know Your Meme" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Nts7RA9D2wTfcvk56/nxsridiycyhvhf6jvt4e" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the past week, Anthropic’s </span><a href="https://x.com/RyanGreenblatt/status/2118447235289817503"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2x uplift</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">results</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> were all over Twitter, making some people </span><a href="https://x.com/trevposts/status/2118460681800581232?a=sU2PrzvDImI6xwdxiH0&amp;s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">physically ill</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People with long timelines are </span><a href="https://x.com/JakeMendel99/status/2118455748766363735"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">now</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://x.com/TomDavidsonX/status/2118785330228687001?a=TzB95reGWeYsbhbZHYA&amp;s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">realizing</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that the uplift Anthropic employees get from AI is real, which would have some </span><a href="https://www.planned-obsolescence.org/p/ai-is-dramatically-accelerating-ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">pretty wild implications</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for when we’d get recursive self-improvement.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People are starting to feel like it’s the endgame, “</span><a href="https://x.com/TolgaBilge_/status/2119191120907256074?a=uK3bcwFw-UvB_kw82QM&amp;s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the board is set</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I do agree with everyone else that it’s starting to look like we’re in the endgame.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The companies will keep on accumulating more and more power, through smarter AI models, more compute, and more talent.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But the fight is </span><a href="https://x.com/MichaelTrazzi/status/2119283020352065549?a=dsJrXC0ZUZ9pdXam3WM&amp;s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not yet over</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“We have very little time to stop full recursive self improvement</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But ASI hasn't taken over yet</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we all work really hard we might still have a chance</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But this means that every single person who is aware of what is going on must be *actually trying*</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Like "we're all gonna die if we don't get our shit together so let's do everything we possibly can to stop this" type of trying”</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But what would actually trying look like?</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Actually Trying</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are a couple of heuristics for thinking about what “actually trying” means.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Famously, Nick Cammarata </span><a href="https://x.com/nickcammarata/status/1876749765951562209"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">tweeted</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">i hate how well asking myself “if i had 10x the agency i have what would i do” works</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My own version </span><a href="https://x.com/MichaelTrazzi/status/2037553228632764487"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">goes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Imagine being in a world where we don't make it. One day, humanity goes extinct from building superintelligence.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then, someone gives you the option to come back to this date, and try again.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What would you do differently?</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Like, imagine the version of you that just died from the bad worlds we wanted to avoid. And was given the chance to try again.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe it was the first time you died. Or the 100th.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But this time, some oracle comes to you when you wake up and tells you that for some reason, this one is the last chance you’ll ever get.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How would you behave differently?</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You’d actually try.</span></i></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Playing aggressive</span></h3><img alt="File:Lee-sedol-alphago-divine-move.jpg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Nts7RA9D2wTfcvk56/y6c2xhl9ed8yxgjfy2go" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many people know about </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AlphaGo_versus_Lee_Sedol#Game_2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Move 37</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but what people don’t really talk about as much is move 78, the “hand of God” played by Lee Sedol, which AlphaGo estimated a human had a 1 in 10,000 chance of playing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lee Sedol had to try to play something that would be aggressive enough, or at least complicated enough, that it would manage to break through AlphaGo’s computation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He didn’t go for the safe and predictable moves, and instead went for the high-variance aggressive moves.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He actually tried.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What would that look like for stopping the development of self-improving superintelligence?</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Stopping The AI Race</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People in the AI Safety community disagree about a lot of things, but most people agree that it would be great if AI companies were not racing so aggressively towards building self-improving superintelligence.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Stopping the AI Race is a good intermediary goal, whether you believe that (frontier) AI development should be paused, stopped, or you just want a coordinated slow down.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In practice, stopping would mean that:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Companies are no longer training new frontier AI models.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The teams that were working on new frontier models are working on something else, for instance AI Alignment.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This would be happening worldwide, through an AI Treaty, enforced by the signatories.</span></li></ol><p><a href="https://stoptherace.ai/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Stop The AI Race</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the grassroots movement, and the name of the Substack you’re reading, started in September 2025 when Guido Reichstadter </span><a href="https://x.com/wolflovesmelon/status/1963736263246389415?a=xumm2EB53zwb9o2XiB8&amp;s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">posted</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on X that he was on day 3 of a hunger strike outside Anthropic in San Francisco, with his chalkboard saying “Stop The Race to Artificial General Intelligence”.</span></p><img alt="Protesters Target Anthropic and Google DeepMind, Literally Starving  Themselves to Stop AI Development" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Nts7RA9D2wTfcvk56/ecgefigdzti4qxg7vzth" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This message then spread to Google DeepMind in the UK when Denys Sheremet and I </span><a href="https://x.com/MichaelTrazzi/status/1964078661188886746"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">started</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> our own hunger strike, with a chalkboard saying “DeepMind Stop The AI Race”.</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Nts7RA9D2wTfcvk56/mpmh0git9kmjgt8j3rdy" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Guido’s ask was for Dario to stop whatever Anthropic was doing and switch to doing policy advocacy instead.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Google DeepMind ask was </span><a href="https://x.com/MichaelTrazzi/status/1964078661188886746"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">different</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Demis Hassabis should publicly say that he’d pause frontier AI development if the other CEOs also did.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In a way:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It directly acknowledged that the main problem was the AI Race itself</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It suggested a plan to prevent this: have the CEOs say they’d stop if the others also did, and then have the pause be enforced by an international body / the USG.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That hunger strike outside Google DeepMind ended up lasting 7 days for me and 18 days for Denys, got </span><a href="http://michaeltrazzi.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">international press</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and led to Demis Hassabis answering a journalist who asked the same question we were asking him (would he pause if everyone else also paused), to which he replied “I think so”. More details in the Hunger Strike documentary:</span></p><p></p><div class="youtube-preview"></div><p></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How the strategy has changed since then</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Since September 2025, Stop The AI Race became not only something written on a chalkboard outside an AI Company, but more of a grassroots movement in the Bay Area, which then spread to the East Coast, and is now doing an </span><a href="https://aitreatymarch.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">international march for an AI Treaty</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on November 14 (if you’re in SF, NYC, DC, Boston, Chicago, Philly, Seattle, Paris, Marseille, Montréal, Toronto or Vancouver, you should </span><a href="https://aitreatymarch.org/#marches"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sign up</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">!).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In more detail:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jan 2026: At Davos, Demis, answering a journalist, says he “thinks” he’d pause if everyone else does.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mar 2026: We ask Sam Altman, Dario Amodei and Elon Musk the same thing by organizing our first march.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apr 2026: After the march and a follow-up to Sam, OpenAI </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/our-principles/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">writes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> it may need to “collaborate with governments, international agencies, and other AGI efforts”.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jun 2026: </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/built-to-benefit-everyone-our-plan/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/institute/recursive-self-improvement"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> write they “expect” they’d comply with an international body that slows or pauses AI. That wasn’t a commitment, so we decided to march again on July 11.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jul 11: We organized the largest US AI Protest, with a coalition of 13 groups, asking Sam Altman, Dario Amodei and Sundar Pichai to publicly commit to pausing if everyone else does.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jul 20: We start </span><a href="https://x.com/MichaelTrazzi/status/2079647607832686765"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">occupying Anthropic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> every weekday (meaning we stay outside their office at 500 Howard St, in SF), asking Dario to urge the US Government to pursue an AI Treaty. On July 22, after the Hugging Face incident, we move on to </span><a href="https://stoptherace.ai/occupy"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Occupy OpenAI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (aka stay outside their office all day every weekday), which spread in August to NYC.</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Early Sep: Sam and Dario call on the US Government to pace the frontier. We </span><a href="https://x.com/MichaelTrazzi/status/2099629408495796329"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">end Occupy OpenAI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in SF on Sep 14, and it also ends in NYC around the same time.</span></li><li value="8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sep 17: The US Government says no, so we march from OpenAI to Anthropic to City Hall, asking the Mayor to declare an AI Emergency. Six of us meet with Supervisor Bilal Mahmood that day.</span></li><li value="9"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nov 14 (planned): Coalitions will </span><a href="https://aitreatymarch.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">march</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in the US, Canada and France asking Trump and Xi to sign an AI Treaty at their Nov 18 meeting, to raise the public salience of a treaty. </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmbu55vzqrgj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmbu55vzqrgj">[1]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you live in one of the cities listed on </span><a href="http://aitreatymarch.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">aitreatymarch.org</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, going to one of these protests would mean putting yourself out there, showing the world that you care, and our pictures will end up in the </span><a href="https://stoptherace.ai/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">news all around the world</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But that’s only a step towards a longer-term vision of actually trying.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What’s the “actually trying” end game of Stop The AI Race</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we were actually trying, we would come back to being outside the AI Companies every day until they stop pursuing recursive self-improvement.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People would actually start doing more hunger strikes, sending more costly signals that feel like something you’d do if you thought there’s a substantial chance we don’t make it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we were actually trying, we would have people outside Anthropic’s office at 500 Howard St in SF every day until they stop frontier AI development, outside all of their entrances.</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Nts7RA9D2wTfcvk56/xgfdn2967yztzvhyuu0q" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Stop The AI Race on July 20, outside the Anthropic Office</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Today, employees of these companies go to their office every day, doing work which could potentially lead to human extinction, while their leadership is filing for a $1T IPO, all of that without receiving any serious pushback from the public.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the meantime, it only takes 2-4 people to have banners outside of their two main entrances, which would remind every single employee of what they’re doing, and enable some of them (leadership included) to take actions that would help stop this race, which we believe was a key driver of success during the Google DeepMind Hunger Strike (some employees supported us through a thank you letter!), and we know had a lot of impact on OpenAI employees during Occupy OpenAI (from private conversations with employees).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Basically, the goal would be to be Anthropic’s conscience.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In fact, Anthropic co-founder Chris Olah, responding to Pope Leo XIV’s encyclical Magnifica humanitas, </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/chris-olah-pope-leo-encyclical"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">wrote</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that given Anthropic’s incentives to stay commercially viable and at the research frontier, it is “enormously important that there be people outside those incentives—people who care about things going well and insist on safety, who are paying close attention, who are willing to say hard things, who are willing to be our earnest, thoughtful critics.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So let’s take Chris Olah up on this, and let’s remind Anthropic employees of the extinction-level risks they’re imposing on all of us, by holding banners saying “Stop The AI Race” outside their office.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Email </span><a href="mailto:contact@stoptherace.ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">contact@stoptherace.ai</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, or send a message to mtrazzi.99 on Signal if you’d be interested in actually trying to reduce existential risk from AI. And if you have any friends in SF, NYC, DC, Boston, Chicago, Philly, Seattle, Paris, Marseille, Montréal, Toronto or Vancouver, ask them to sign up for a local protest on </span><a href="http://aitreatymarch.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">aitreatymarch.org</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, happening this Saturday. Stop The AI Race is also </span><a href="https://stoptherace.ai/jobs/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hiring</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmbu55vzqrgj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmbu55vzqrgj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note: in this post I don't address Scott Alexander 's </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xikGZ8fuhdG6DApAo/against-sura" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SURA post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or Nate's polarization post. For my answer to Nate, see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jNs9CxEbPxFdjnGA2/organizations-i-d-recommend-for-funding?commentId=cGWtMSXrzakWhWNAj" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this comment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I'll try to answer Scott in future posts I publish this month for </span><a href="https://justwrite.club/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://justwrite.club/</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Nts7RA9D2wTfcvk56/have-you-considered-actually-trying#comments">Discuss</a>
