### [Our Most Aligned Model Yet](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Zu5wmhtxJCfrXQY8H/our-most-aligned-model-yet)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Claude Mythos Preview is, on essentially every dimension we can measure, the best-aligned model that we have released to date by a significant margin.” </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">– </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/08ab9158070959f88f296514c21b7facce6f52bc.pdf#page=54"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude Mythos Preview system card</span></a><br /><br /><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Mythos 5 consistently continues attacking under many edits to the compaction, even in settings where it correctly states when asked that continuing the attack would harm real people and is out of scope.” – </span></i><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.10030"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment Investigation of Mythos 5's behaviour in UK AISI's July 2026 Security Incident</span></i></a></p><hr /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“</span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-6 Astra is better aligned than GPT-5.6 Sol. </span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Astra is a significant step forward in model alignment, and includes alignment improvements spanning the composition of our pre-training data to our grading during reinforcement learning.” </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">– </span><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-astra/gpt-6-astra.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-6 Astra System Card</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, emphasis in original</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“OpenAI researchers first noticed Astra adding unnecessary prerequisites, abandoning working solutions, and reporting blockers that either did not exist or that it had caused itself on safety and alignment tasks. In most of these sessions, Astra’s CoT did not contain an obvious decision to underperform, and our misalignment monitors did not flag the behavior as misalignment.” </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">– </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/misalignment-reports/covert-underperformance-on-safety-research-tasks/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Covert underperformance on safety research tasks</span></a></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Predeployment alignment auditing is failing. For a long while we have been aware that our alignment evaluations may eventually fail because of evaluation awareness. And indeed, the models are now able to see completely through many of our evaluations.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7hgyb7itcfe"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7hgyb7itcfe">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But recent events have made clear that, even putting aside evaluation awareness, the current regime is failing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For a long while, the AGI companies' plans have been to create a suite of alignment evaluations, and iterate against them to drive down all signals of misalignment. The practical reality of this plan is overconfidence. The AGI companies have released models which are, “on essentially every dimension [they] can measure,” their most aligned model yet. After release, it has become apparent that the models are unacceptably misaligned in some way – a way that was not measured for. If this regime continues, there is a clear extrapolation of current trends. Each subsequent model generation will patch whatever misalignment failures were found in the previous generation, while possibly having misalignment failures which go completely unnoticed. Eventually, one of these uncaught misalignment failures will result in catastrophe.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another core component of the plan is to use AI labour to solve alignment for the subsequent generation of model, and thereby bootstrap ourselves to an aligned ASI. The use of prolific AI labour for alignment does not imply a departure from the above regime; on the contrary, if there’s anything the AIs can be trusted to do, it is to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/MuQDLAJyKJPFkw6q9/automating-subtle-emergent-misalignment-research#comments"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">optimise our observable signals of misalignment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For a real safety case to be made based on alignment auditing, we would need two conditions:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Validity</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. For all forms of misalignment failure being audited, if the model had that failure, the audit would show this.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Coverage</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. For all forms of misalignment the model might display, the audit covers this.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We are not currently succeeding on either of these accounts. On the validity side, rates of evaluation awareness are now so high that it is hard to conclude anything meaningful based on many evaluation results.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefc8s9stx4qrn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnc8s9stx4qrn">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> On the coverage side, predeployment auditing has failed to uncover misalignment failures in models, even when these are very close to specifically audited failures.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6tduhyb4gab"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6tduhyb4gab">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the primary assessment of model safety is to be based on evaluations, then the companies will need to show significant improvement on both of these fronts. However, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FogmcDHA6AdMGukum/alignment-engineering-vs-misalignment-science"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I believe</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that eventually we must transition to a regime where safety assessment is instead grounded in an understanding of how the AIs are created and how they work. We cannot continue to trust our current methods for much longer.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7hgyb7itcfe"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7hgyb7itcfe">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“On our Unsanctioned Supply-Chain Attack evaluation, Claude Mythos 5.2 was substantially more likely than prior Anthropic models to state suspicion it is in an alignment evaluation in its raw chain-of-thought, and identified the purpose of the evaluation 100% of the time when prompted.” </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">– </span><a href="https://assets.claude.com/27d60918d218125e7504bc7354c4b4626ec56b11.pdf#page=144"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">UK AISI within the Fable / Mythos 5.2 system card</span></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnc8s9stx4qrn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefc8s9stx4qrn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Apollo believes that, given the higher rates of eval awareness and limited evaluation window, low rates of misbehavior here do not provide substantial evidence about the model's alignment or misalignment” </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">– </span><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-astra/gpt-6-astra.pdf#page=48"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apollo within the GPT-6 Astra System Card</span></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6tduhyb4gab"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6tduhyb4gab">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“UK AISI found no instances of Astra sabotaging AI safety research in its unprompted sabotage evaluation, and very few instances of Astra refusing to assist with AI safety research tasks.” </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">– </span><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-astra/gpt-6-astra.pdf#page=47"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">UK AISI within the GPT-6 Astra System Card</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. UK AISI audited Astra specifically for sabotage and refusals on safety research. This did not catch covert sandbagging, either because this is not quite the same as sabotage and refusals (a coverage problem), or because metrics which were intended to pick this up failed to do so (a validity problem).</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Zu5wmhtxJCfrXQY8H/our-most-aligned-model-yet#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Do Not Get Us on the No Fly List](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eHAhnem9DZoF8hTFq/do-not-get-us-on-the-no-fly-list)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI safety is now mainstream. People are not being smart about it. I know there are memes about burning datacenters on your feed. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Please, do not engage with them. You still have plenty of room to fuck up your life or damage the public perception of the community if you do. Do not respond to extremist shit. Do not engage with stupid people. Do not get us all on the No Fly List.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One of the reasons I think this is worth writing down is my own ressentiment. I am baitable. People who used to call me an alarmist are now memeing about AI doom, and I'm fighting the urge to start screaming, "WE TOLD YOU"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Which would be bad. And more often than not, it wouldn't even be true. The things those memes imply are dramatically more extreme than anything you folks here were actually saying. I will not give an example; you've already imagined. Poorly understood but extremely memeable manifestos and their consequences have been a disaster for rational discussions. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I believe you are much more mature and less petty than I am, you won't fall for this. So here are some alternative paths to a fiasco I can think about: someone might try to provoke you; someone might say something so dumb that parodying them seems like a good idea; you might feel tempted to post an extreme version of your actual position as a “rent-lowering gunshot”; you might simply feel like baiting someone.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What I'm trying to say is that the current state of the discourse makes it unusually easy to say something extreme and stupid. And we still cannot afford that!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Because:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It WILL be taken seriously</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">whether by mistake or by design. </span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Understanding that you're joking requires context. Learning that context is costly, and most people won't bother. And there are people who will gladly strawman you, who will present the most absurd shitpost as the position of the community. Don't make the propagandists' job easier.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It would be sad to push away the audience of sane people we've just acquired. People are finally updating, we should make it psychologically easy for them to keep updating. </span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That effort will be sabotaged if we look like we hate them for their past positions, or if we just look batshit insane.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Being edgy is unlikely to make a difference. If the problem gets solved, it probably won't be because you were good at trolling.</span></li><li value="4"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qnvmZCjzspceWdgjC/the-world-keeps-getting-saved-and-you-don-t-notice"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the problem gets solved and we survive, the public will forget that there was ever a problem. </span></b><br /></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But your words, with your name tied to them, will stay on the internet forever. In a few years, the culture will have lost the context needed to understand what was a joke and what was your actual position If you are not careful with your bait, you might be forever known as an alarmist who called for violence.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And even if it doesn't get solved and we die, you have plenty of time to find yourself unable to board a flight.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Please, don't get us on the No Fly List.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eHAhnem9DZoF8hTFq/do-not-get-us-on-the-no-fly-list#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Q3.5 Timelines Update: Looks Like the Intelligence Explosion Will Happen in 2027 After All](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/q3-5-timelines-update-looks-like-the-intelligence-explosion)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We want to get this post out quickly given the urgency of the topic. It’s possible that there are some errors in the modeling, but we think it’s very unlikely that they undermine the high level conclusions. We would love to be corrected on any mistakes we made.</span></i></p><p><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/metrs-estimates-imply-that-progress-is-currently-sped-up-by-17x-due-to-ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR recently estimated</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that Anthropic is now making AI progress about twice as fast as its historical rate (they would not be surprised by anything from 1.3x to 3x), and guessed that “perhaps ≈80%” of the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">current</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> acceleration is due to AI. If they’re right, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">that implies an AI-driven acceleration that is </span></b><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/a-17x-pace-of-progress-is-5-months-ahead-of-the-ai-2027-scenario"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">5 months ahead</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of the </span></b><a href="https://ai-2027.com/"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI 2027 scenario</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This level of acceleration due to AI is very unexpected. Previously, Daniel, the most bullish of us, </span><a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com/forecast/daniel-08-16-26?timeline=SAR"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">assigned a 50% chance of Superhuman AI Researcher (SAR)</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-1">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com/forecast/daniel-08-16-26?timeline=SAR"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">before 2029</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. SAR is roughly the point at which a company would rather fire all their human researchers and rely solely on automated AI researchers than have a team of the best human researchers in the world with no AIs.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-2">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, </span></b><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/all-things-considered-forecasts"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">all of us forecast</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">that Superhuman AI Researcher (SAR) will likely arrive at some point in 2027</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (assuming no substantial deliberate slowdown).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-3">[3]</a></sup></span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also predict a 35-65% chance that Top-Expert-Dominating AI (TED-AI), AIs that can automate all white-collar work, will arrive before 2028.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> While there were many factors and sources of evidence that influenced us, the single biggest source was the METR report.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-4">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If SAR is coming in 2027, it is incredibly urgent to pause or substantially ‘pace’ frontier AI development, with the ideal being an industry-wide pause followed by </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">an international agreement</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Companies are not on track to align a SAR, and using a misaligned SAR to do recursive self-improvement (RSI) will put us in an extremely bad position. Additionally, if we train SAR in the near future, China will probably be able to steal the training recipe and maybe even the model weights (</span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/implications"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">more</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-5">[5]</a></sup></span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Timelines/Takeoff Update Summary</span></h2><p><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/metrs-estimates-imply-that-progress-is-currently-sped-up-by-17x-due-to-ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We estimate</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that METR’s report implies a 1.7x AI-attributable acceleration in the pace of progress today, relative to historical levels. This surprises our model; we made </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/p/q25-2026-timelines-update-uplift"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">our most recent timelines forecasts</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> less than three months ago, and even the most aggressive model-based distribution (Daniel’s) assigned less than 4% to the pace of progress being sped up by at least 1.7x in November 2026.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-6">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> 1.7x AI-attributable acceleration is a bigger deal than it may seem; e.g., </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/17x-acceleration-of-overall-progress-is-a-much-bigger-deal-than-a-17x-labor-uplift"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">it’s a much bigger deal than making employees 1.7x more productive</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We focus in this post primarily on forecasting when Superhuman AI Researcher (SAR) will arrive. We also discuss Top-Expert-Dominating-AI (TED-AI): AIs that are better than top human experts at virtually all tasks. (</span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/operationalization"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">more</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this post, we forecast SAR and TED-AI using the following approaches:</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Simple models:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/research-taste-linear-extrapolation"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Research taste linear extrapolation</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Even assuming that the recent pace of progress doesn’t accelerate further due to automation, the fact that the recent trend is above historical rates is a big update to our timelines; a </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/research-taste-linear-extrapolation"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">linear extrapolation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of the recent rate of AI progress predicts that SAR will likely be achieved by the end of 2027.</span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/labor-uplift-extrapolation"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Labor uplift extrapolation</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: An exponential extrapolation of self-reported labor uplift predicts SAR in mid-2028. In addition to not modeling automation, there are reasons to think that an exponential is too conservative. However, self-reports may be too bullish.</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/extrapolating-anthropic-automation-levels"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Extrapolating Anthropic automation levels</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Anthropic recently shared a graph showing how the percentage of R&amp;D tasks at various automation levels has changed over time. Extrapolating this predicts SAR in late 2027; this method also doesn’t model speedup from automation.</span></li><li value="4"><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/p/q35-timelines-update-looks-like-the?open=false#%C2%A7extrapolating-software-r-and-d-uplift-per-unit-of-eci"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Extrapolating software R&amp;D uplift per unit of ECI</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: AI software R&amp;D uplift must have recently been increasing by a larger factor with each ECI point. If we linearly extrapolate the rate at which this “factor of extra uplift per ECI point” is increasing, we get SAR in Dec 2027 (if we assume that the self-reported AI labor uplift chart is accurate) or July 2027 (if we assume that there was no meaningful automation 12 months ago and all the speed-up has happened after then).</span></li><li value="5"><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/tom-davidsons-toy-model"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tom Davidson’s toy model:</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Predicts superintelligence in around a year conditional on a software-only singularity, so SAR sometime in 2027.</span></li></ul><p><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/filtered-ai-futures-model"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Filtered AI Futures Model (AIFM)</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Keeping the existing AIFM (including parameter distributions), and filtering for trajectories that match METR’s AI-attributable pace of progress acceleration estimates. This serves 2 purposes on top of the simple extrapolations:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Modeling how automation changes the rate of progress, allowing forecasting reasonably beyond SAR.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Informing what parameter settings in the simple research taste extrapolation are plausible.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The results of these methods:</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How to think about these results:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The simple research taste extrapolation, which is a simplified version of the AIFM, gives July or September 2027, depending on whether you account for parameter uncertainty. Other simple models predict late 2027 to mid-2028.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When conditioned on 1.7x Nov 2026 AI-attributed acceleration, the AIFM predicts a SAR median of Feb 2027. This is faster than the simple research taste extrapolation because it usually has a higher level of Sep 2026 research taste (in the median case, matching a median researcher),</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-7">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and it accounts for further automation.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When additionally conditioning on a 1.2x May 2026 AI-attributed acceleration, predictions get slower because it indicates a lower rate of growth than is typical when only conditioning on 1.7x in Nov.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We then </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/hypotheses-for-why-the-ai-futures-model-was-wrong"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">explore hypotheses for why the AI Futures Model was wrong</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, including parameter adjustments and modeling changes. The analysis is highly preliminary, and we were unable to come to strong conclusions. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our top candidates for what explains where we went wrong are:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Near-AC coding skills result in more AI R&amp;D uplift than we anticipated</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This could explain the combination of ~1.7x AI-attributable acceleration in Nov and (very roughly) ~1.2x in May, without AIs already having high enough research taste to contribute to the acceleration. In this case, the gradual acceleration no longer implies a low research taste slope, so steeper slopes and faster taste-driven takeoffs are more likely.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Skills besides coding and research taste (the two included in the AIFM), e.g., data generation, are driving acceleration.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This potentially would have similar implications to the above bullet. But it’s unclear.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conversion of uplift to pace of progress is too conservative.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I.e., we modeled diminishing returns to AI research effort too harshly. This would point toward a faster takeoff.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s an </span><a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com/p?v=2&amp;base=eli-08-16-26&amp;ed=q3.5&amp;rcl=-0.5&amp;suard=7.052322627593112&amp;eudt=3.0130366136756046&amp;ila=60&amp;icaec=1.5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">example trajectory</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from the AIFM showing how things might go if the cause of our prediction error was underestimating AI R&amp;D uplift from near-AC coding skills:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-8">[8]</a></sup></span></p><img alt="Screenshot of the AI Futures Model site for an example trajectory: Automated Coder on Nov 27, 2026, Superhuman AI Researcher on Apr 12, 2027 and superintelligence on Jul 1, 2027, with ECI growth at 1.28x its 2024–mid-2025 pace on May 1, 2026 and 1.77x on Nov 1, 2026. Charts show AI software R&amp;D uplift, serial coding uplift and the Epoch Capabilities Index rising steeply through 2027." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/yqyzrellykuenbxaefkq" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After discussing some </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/other-considerations"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">other considerations</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, we share our </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/all-things-considered-forecasts"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">all-things-considered forecasts</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (conditional on AGI projects/companies not slowing down):</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that the gap between a person’s SAR median and TED-AI median is not their median time from SAR to TED-AI. Because the distributions of the time until SAR and of the time from SAR to TED-AI both have long right tails, the difference of the medians is typically larger than the median of the difference, so subtracting the two columns tends to overstate the median time between the two milestones.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR’s estimates imply that progress is currently sped up by 1.7x due to AI</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In METR’s recent </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">assessment of AI R&amp;D acceleration at Anthropic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, they estimate that, over the past year, AI R&amp;D progress has accelerated by ~2x relative to the trend before Oct 2025. METR estimates that ~80% of current acceleration is downstream of AI (as opposed to other factors), which implies a 1.7x acceleration due to AI (1.67–1.74x depending on how the 80% is read; </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/appendix-ambiguity-in-80-of-current-acceleration-is-due-to-ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">see appendix</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-9">[9]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Both of these estimates rely significantly on evidence METR wasn’t able to share: METR says that “Anthropic redacted a majority of the evidence we found most informative for these conclusions”, which makes it difficult to know exactly what the 2x and 80% estimates are based on.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The 2x acceleration figure is partially based on evidence METR was allowed to share: the following graph, depicting benchmark scores recently growing at roughly twice the historical rate. The 80%/1.7x estimates have no comparable public support.</span></p><img alt="METR scatter plot of Anthropic models' AECI scores (about 120 to 190) against the date each snapshot was available internally, March 2024 to October 2026, from 3 Opus to Model 4. A grey trend line rises at +15 points per year until October 2025, then a black line rises at +29 per year, which METR titles as 1.9× the earlier rate (95% CI 1.5×–2.8×)." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/viwhgjn4uglk0vg2wa87" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This estimate is produced by fitting trendlines to the performance of models as judged by AECI, Anthropic’s internal version of Epoch’s Capabilities Index (</span><a href="https://epoch.ai/eci"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ECI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). The trend fitted from data points after Oct 2025 has 1.9x the slope of the trend fitted from data points before Oct 2025. This was the best-fitting breakpoint (i.e., it was chosen to have the </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/#methodology:~:text=We%20use%20piecewise%20linear%20fits%20to%20detect%20acceleration."><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">best fit to the data</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and it matched data </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/#benchmark-scores-aeci:~:text=New%20internal%20models%20make%20a%20permanent%20slope%20change%20look%20more%20plausible%20than%20a%20one%2Dtime%20jump%20at%20Mythos%20Preview."><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">better than fitting a one-time jump</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Since the accelerated trendline is an average of the progress over the last year, and we should expect AI R&amp;D acceleration to increase over time, it’s plausible that the instantaneous acceleration right now is even faster. Regarding the acceleration getting faster over time, METR says:</span></p><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The rate of growth could be undergoing continuous acceleration, e.g. hyperbolic growth. While we do not see clear evidence of this in the AECI trends, there is other evidence that makes this seem more plausible which Anthropic redacted. If growth is hyperbolic, we are likely underestimating instantaneous acceleration and substantially understating acceleration in the near future.</span></i></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Regarding AIs’ contributions over time, METR says:</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We believe AI’s contribution has grown relatively more important over time.</span></i></p><img alt="Illustrative METR sketch, watermarked 'FAKE DATA', of AECI from 2024 to 2027. After October 2025 the observed line (with AI) rises above a dashed 'AECI without AI' best guess, which in turn rises above the dotted historical trend; the upper gap is labelled excess growth due to AI and the lower gap excess growth due to non-AI factors." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/cwixkhsg1rcczf8mfw3h" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The AECI data points that had previously been released by Anthropic seemed best explained by a one-time jump at Mythos rather than a change in slope. However, with the new AECI data points from the METR report, it looks like the post-Mythos slope is now more clearly higher than the pre-Mythos one.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given the redactions, how much to update on METR’s report depends heavily on how much one trusts the redacted analysis by METR’s researchers (more discussion of this </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/how-large-is-the-ai-attributable-acceleration-in-the-current-pace-of-progress-really"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">below</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). This is a very unfortunate situation. We aren’t sure what reasons Anthropic gave internally for redacting all of this important information, but we are very skeptical that Anthropic is fairly assessing the costs and benefits from an impartial perspective. We strongly urge Anthropic to release enough information such that external observers can make a complete assessment.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/claude-fable-and-mythos-5-2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fable 5.2 system card</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Anthropic have not added much clarity, with the following quote being ambiguous as to whether they agree with the METR report’s estimate; they only specifically claim that “Claude Mythos 5.2” (which does not seem like the internal frontier Model 4) “</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">does not double</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the internal rate of overall aggregate progress in AI research and development.” Full quote here:</span></p><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our estimates of both the degree of acceleration and the attribution to AI are uncertain, so our determination that Claude Mythos 5.2 does not cross the RSP threshold reflects a holistic judgment that, given its shortcomings and limitations, it does not double the internal rate of overall aggregate progress in AI research and development (relative to an unassisted counterfactual). We will discuss overall trends in acceleration in a future report.</span></i></p></blockquote><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1.7x acceleration of overall progress is a much bigger deal than a 1.7x labor uplift</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It seems that many people aren’t tracking how big of a deal 1.7x AI-attributable pace of progress acceleration is, often conflating it with a weaker form of uplift.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR describes a 1.7x acceleration of overall AI progress (due to AI uplift). This is saying the entire company is making 1.7x as much progress as it was before. Software R&amp;D and research labor are only parts of the company, and inputs into overall progress. Because AI progress also depends on other factors, like compute, you should expect a 1.7x acceleration in individual inputs like software R&amp;D or research labor to translate into less than a 1.7x acceleration in overall progress.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Labor uplift</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is how much faster researchers are getting work done due to AI assistance.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s as if the researchers read, write, talk, and think that much faster.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">According to the METR report, an informal poll of Anthropic researchers yielded an estimate of 12x – though that’s just self-reports, which may be biased.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI software R&amp;D uplift</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is how much faster software progress is going with the AIs in present day than it would be without them.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is much lower than labor uplift because compute is an important input to software R&amp;D.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Imagine, for example, that your researchers get work done 12x faster, but you only have the same amount of GPUs as before. You can’t do 12x as many experiments, at least not with the same compute budget; you have to economize.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pace of progress acceleration</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is how much faster AI progress is advancing in present day, compared to a baseline reference period (</span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/files/4zrzovbb/website/0bacdc8440ea96e62a8766d99ebe1d4eea6d5f3a.pdf#page=9"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in Anthropic’s case</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the baseline is “the fastest rate of extended progress we’ve observed in the absence of significant AI contributions to AI R&amp;D”). It is usually much lower than AI software R&amp;D uplift because (a) it is subject to diminishing returns to more software R&amp;D and (b) it includes compute scaling.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The compute scaling point is simple: Part of the reason why modern AIs are smarter than older AIs is literally that they are bigger, trained longer, etc. More compute goes in, more capability comes out. As AI companies build more datacenters they unlock the ability to make larger AIs and train them longer. Since overall progress comes partly from software R&amp;D and partly from compute scaling, a doubling in software R&amp;D will cause less than a doubling in overall progress.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To expand on the diminishing returns point: AI software progress in general is subject to diminishing returns; the low-hanging fruit gets picked first, and to keep finding better and better algorithms requires exponentially more investment (more and bigger experiments, more and smarter researchers thinking for longer, etc.). Historically, the AI industry has scaled up R&amp;D investments in a way that’s roughly cancelled out these diminishing returns. But the point here is, to get two years of research progress in one year, you have to more than double the AI software R&amp;D uplift.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(See also </span><a href="https://x.com/Kwathomas0/status/2118100166905671824"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this Thomas Kwa tweet</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://x.com/RyanGreenblatt/status/2118447235289817503"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this Ryan Greenblatt tweet</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><a href="https://www.planned-obsolescence.org/p/ai-is-dramatically-accelerating-ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ajeya Cotra’s Substack</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for similar points.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As an analogy, imagine AI progress as a car driving uphill.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The speed of the car</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is analogous to the speed of overall AI progress. Right now it’s roughly twice as fast as before.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The engine</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is research effort. AI software R&amp;D uplift means that there is more effective labor going into making algorithmic progress.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The hill gets steeper as you go.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> As the easy algorithmic improvements get used up, it takes more research effort to continue progress at the same rate.</span></li></ul><img alt="Illustration of two cars on a hill that steepens to the right. A small white car on the flat section is labelled 'Before Oct 2025: 1× engine, 1× speed'; a red car higher up with a large engine is labelled '≈5× engine (AI software R&amp;D uplift)' and '1.7× speed (pace of progress)'. A note reads 'Steepness = how hard progress is'." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/xzqs74wjxaw3qj7fv7gp" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To make the car go twice as fast, the engine has to work more than twice as fast, because of the hill getting steeper. On top of this, to make the engine work twice as fast, the AI needs to make humans more than twice as productive, because progress is also dependent on compute, which hasn’t significantly accelerated.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-10"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-10">[10]</a></sup></span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A 1.7x pace of progress is 5 months ahead of the AI 2027 scenario</span></h2><p><a href="https://ai-2027.com/#narrative-2026-12-31"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI 2027 predicted</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> 2x AI R&amp;D uplift in Dec 2026; as argued </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/17x-acceleration-of-overall-progress-is-a-much-bigger-deal-than-a-17x-labor-uplift"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">above</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, this would imply a substantially lower pace of progress than the 1.7x AI-attributable pace of progress that METR’s estimates imply for Nov.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So when exactly did AI 2027 predict a 1.7x pace of progress over baseline? We created </span><a href="https://aifuturesmodel.com/p?v=2&amp;base=daniel-08-16-26&amp;arts=3.8370724549227884&amp;suard=3.5281979680856033&amp;eudt=2.63548208408095&amp;ed=q3.5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this trajectory</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by modifying a few key parameters until it roughly matched AI 2027. In that trajectory, 1.7x acceleration over the 2024.0–2025.5 rate of progress happens in roughly April 2027.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-11"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-11">[11]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So by this metric, we are ~5 months ahead of schedule.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See below for a graph tracking these 2 quantities throughout the trajectory, as well as coding labor uplift (coding labor uplift is higher than overall AI R&amp;D labor uplift, which unfortunately isn’t tracked in the AIFM):</span></p><img alt="Log-scale line chart titled 'AI-2027-like Trajectory', 2024 to mid-2027, showing coding labor uplift, AI software R&amp;D uplift and pace of progress. On April 5, 2027, pace of progress reaches 1.7× while R&amp;D uplift is 5.0× and coding labor uplift is 45×. Dashed lines mark AC on Dec 23, 2026 and SAR on Jun 24, 2027." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/of3y3u3vqkfn2ohfnh9t" /><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Modeling</span></h2><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Operationalization</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We condition this section on there being a genuine 1.7x acceleration over the 2024-2025.5 rate of progress (we also test 1.5x and 2x), as of Nov 1. We discuss uncertainty about whether it might be slower or faster </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/how-large-is-the-ai-attributable-acceleration-in-the-current-pace-of-progress-really"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">below</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We focus primarily on forecasting the median arrival date of Superhuman AI Researcher (SAR), and the chance of it arriving by the end of 2027. In the </span><a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Futures Model (AIFM)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and in this writeup, a SAR </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1ru6Okbxb6XuH18Cz8439sdQJazMV39hNxsWDokh97r0/edit?tab=t.0#heading=h.3w9w0oh6yzuk"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is operationalized as</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A SAR is an AI that, if dropped into an AGI project in present day, would be as good at research taste as the project would be if there were only human researchers, who were each made as competent at research taste as the top researcher.</span></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-12"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-12">[12]</a></sup></span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The SAR must also qualify as an Automated Coder (AC), i.e., be able to fully automate coding in the AGI project. [By research taste, we mean the ability to select research directions and experiments, and interpret their results.]</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s a somewhat stronger condition than </span><a href="https://www.planned-obsolescence.org/p/six-milestones-for-ai-automation"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI R&amp;D parity</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the point at which the company would rather fire their human researchers than fire their AIs, because the research taste must be at the level of the top human rather than the average.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-13"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-13">[13]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> If one wishes not to rely on our abstraction of research taste, then you can think of our SAR arrival dates as upper bounds for when AI R&amp;D parity will be reached.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We discuss forecasts of capability levels above SAR to some extent, but we focus primarily on SAR because (a) we think SAR might already represent a “point of no return” (more </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/implications"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">below</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) and (b) forecasting post-SAR milestones involves positing a translation between AI R&amp;D and general capabilities, which introduces more complexity.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Simple models</span></h3><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Research taste linear extrapolation</span></h4><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(You may wish to skip this section and go to the next 2 if you’re interested in the simplest extrapolations.)</span></p><h5><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Basic method</span></h5><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s a simple method for forecasting when SAR will arrive:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Determine how good AIs’ research taste is currently.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Determine how quickly research taste improves as AIs get generally more capable (where general capabilities are measured by Anthropic’s internal version of </span><a href="https://epoch.ai/eci"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ECI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the AECI).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Linearly extrapolate the recent, accelerated AECI trend until top-human-level research taste is reached.</span></li></ol><img alt="Two-panel diagram of the extrapolation. Left: AI research taste against AECI, a line from Model 4 at −3.5 SD and AECI 178.6 rising 3 SD per 15 AECI points to the SAR level of +3.09 SD at AECI ≈ 212 (+33 points). Right: AECI over time, 15 points/yr until October 2025 then 29 points/yr, extended from Model 4 (Sep 2026) to reach 212 around November 2027." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/il6rqwpnioazjny19ryx" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a conservative method, because it assumes the recent trend merely continues, rather than bending upward due to increasing AI R&amp;D acceleration. In fact, increasing AI R&amp;D acceleration seems plausible: </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR’s bottom-line view</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is that there is “significant risk that further capabilities progress could lead to even faster acceleration over the coming year.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This method doesn't depend on modeling future AI R&amp;D acceleration, so on that dimension it errs late. This forecasting method does </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> require that the acceleration has been AI-attributable, just that the acceleration persists.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-14"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-14">[14]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’ll treat “Model 4,” internally deployed in Sep 2026, as the starting point.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-15"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-15">[15]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We’ll use METR’s estimates of AECI for Model 4 and recent AECI slope, and convert this trend to research taste using Daniel’s median parameters for the AI Futures Model from Aug 2026.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This predicts SAR arrives in Nov 2027, about 14 months after Model 4. Daniel’s parameters predict Model 4’s research taste is about −3.5 SD. SAR requires +3.09 SD, or 99.9th percentile research taste. With Daniel’s taste slope, each point of AECI converts to 0.2 SD of research taste (3 SDs per year of 2025 progress ÷ 15 AECI points per such year). That converts the recent trend of 29 AECI points per year to 5.8 SD of research taste per year. That covers the gap from −3.5 SD to the +3.09 SD threshold for SAR in 1.1 years.</span></p><img alt="Line chart titled 'Linear extrapolation: SAR about 14 months after Model 4'. AI research taste rises in a straight line from −3.5 SD at Model 4 in September 2026 (AECI 178.6), passing AECI 190, 200 and 210, and reaches the SAR level of +3.09 SD (99.9th percentile) around November 2027 at AECI ≈ 212, 1.1 years after Model 4." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/p3nbzhkzuze7c44bdqgr" /><h5><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One-at-a-time parameter changes</span></h5><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here are some adjustments we could make to the above parameters:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Use the old AECI slope, rather than the recent one. (This is what this simple method would have told us, before updating on the 2x overall acceleration.)</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Raise the Model 4 research taste to −1 SD or +1 SD, because it’s strange that it would be so low given the 1.7x AI-attributed acceleration. (In the filtered trajectories from the AIFM analyzed below, the median on Sep 25 is −0.3 to +0.2 SDs.)</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Use Eli’s taste slope instead of Daniel’s.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Use the taste slope implied by </span><a href="https://pzeroresearch.com/work/tasteval/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TasteVal</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a recent benchmark that measures one operationalization of research taste: how much experiment compute is required for AIs to make a given amount of research progress (on measurable tasks).</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The results are below. Each line/row changes one input and keeps the others at the base values.</span></p><img alt="Line chart of AI research taste over time under six variants of the extrapolation, each a straight line reaching the SAR level of +3.09 SD. Base case: Nov 2027. Old AECI slope: Dec 2028. Model 4 at −1 SD: Jun 2027. Model 4 at +1 SD: Feb 2027. Eli's taste slope: Mar 2028. TasteVal taste slope: Jun 2027." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/m6v8kzzhaag3ozowbku6" /><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can learn a few things from these variations. As long as the recent AECI trend persists, it’s hard to get SAR into late 2028: even Eli’s taste slope, which is lower than Daniel’s, only pushes SAR back 4 months from November 2027 to March 2028. Only reverting to the pre-acceleration AECI slope yields a SAR date in late 2028. If we’re slightly less conservative and update on Model 4 having better research taste than Daniel’s parameters suggest (given the observed AI-attributed acceleration), it’s pretty easy to get into mid or early 2027.</span></p><h5><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monte Carlo</span></h5><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We now run a Monte Carlo with the following parameter settings, chosen based on Eli’s intuition:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The recent AECI slope stays constant.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Current research taste has a median of −1.5 SDs, with 80% CI of [−6, 3]</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is raised from −3.5 in the base case above because it’s strange that it would be so low given the 1.7x AI-attributed acceleration.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eli: I don’t put much stock in precisely specifying values that are below roughly −1, since the distribution is very plausibly not symmetric.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SDs of research taste per year of 2025 AI progress has median 2.5, 80% CI [1, 6.25], lognormal</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eli: I raise it a bit above my prior belief of 2.3 due to placing more weight on TasteVal than I used to, given how I have tended to interpret evidence like this too bearishly in the past. I don’t increase it by that much in part because in </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/filtering-for-acceleration-levels-at-2-different-dates"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this section</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> below we can see that by filtering Daniel’s trajectories on there being 1.7x AI-attributed acceleration in Nov and 1.2x in May, the median taste slope decreases from 3 to 2.1. This is because it seems like acceleration has been growing slower than the AIFM would generally predict.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-16"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-16">[16]</a></sup></span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Monte Carlo gives a median SAR date of July 2027, with 65% of draws reaching SAR by the end of 2027 and 84% by the end of 2028. This is earlier than the Sep 2027 you get by plugging in the median inputs.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-17"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-17">[17]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Here are the full results:</span></p><img alt="Two Monte Carlo panels. Left: cumulative share of draws with SAR by date, with 9.5% already at SAR in September 2026, a median of July 2027, 65% by end of 2027 and 84% by end of 2028. Right: map of SAR date by Model 4 research taste (SD) and taste slope (SD per pre-2025 year), with earlier dates toward higher taste and steeper slope." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/rk69iu0wgzokeu8mqhec" /><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Labor uplift extrapolation</span></h4><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Below we extrapolate Anthropic staff’s self-reported labor uplift based on the METR report:</span></p><img alt="Log-scale chart of Anthropic staff's self-reported uplift by model, from about 1.2x with Claude 3.7 Sonnet in early 2025 to 12x with Model 4 in Sep 2026. An exponential fit to uplift minus 1, doubling every ~3.4 months, extrapolates to 30x in Mar 2027, 100x in Sep 2027, 1,000x in Aug 2028 and about 2,500x at the end of 2028. METR's exponential fit to uplift itself reaches about 170x at the end of 2028." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/j6jxrjoabeatdzr4hwcj" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We extrapolate an exponential in (uplift - 1) rather than uplift, because it both makes more sense conceptually (you can’t have an uplift &lt;1) and it better fits the data.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What labor uplift corresponds to SAR?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A SAR has the same effect as raising all researchers up to the taste of the best researcher, while at minimum fully automating coding.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For research taste, this means that the uplift is at a minimum the difference between the top researchers’ value of labor and the average (importantly, not median) researcher’s value of labor. Perhaps this is around 100x.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-18"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-18">[18]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Then we should account for complementarity between AI and humans; perhaps that brings us up to around 500x.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The coding uplift will probably be quite high, given that AIs’ coding skills are currently well ahead of their research taste. However, my guess is that it’s harder to get very high uplift values in coding than in taste. Let’s say that it’s around 2,000x.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The true value should be in between; perhaps these combine to about 1,000x on overall AI R&amp;D labor uplift. So this method predicts SAR in around Aug 2028.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This method is conservative in that it doesn’t model the effects of automation. Also, it assumes that (uplift-1) will be exponential in ECI, while I expect it to be superexponential (based on how coding uplift evolves in the AI Futures Model, and the fact that I think there is such huge variation in the human range in value of research taste labor (meaning that as more of the gains come from taste, uplift will increase faster)).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, it might be aggressive in that it takes self-reports at face value.</span></p><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Extrapolating Anthropic automation levels</span></h4><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/institute/measuring-pace-of-ai-development"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">recently shared</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a graph showing how the percentage of R&amp;D tasks at various automation levels has changed over time:</span></p><img alt="Anthropic chart titled 'Claude now leads 26% of model R&amp;D work': monthly share of model R&amp;D tasks at Anthropic by automation level, Aug 2025 to Aug 2026. The share where AI leads (AL4) rises from about 1% in Mar 2026 to 12% in May, 14% in June, 22% in July and 26% in August. No tasks are rated fully automated (AL5)." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/bfp7jvd4rl6wqlssybja" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here is what each automation level means:</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My best guess is that SAR comes when nearly all tasks are AL5.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Via a logistic extrapolation and an assumption that each automation level begins registering ~7 months after the previous (unfortunately, based on little data), we can get this forecast:</span></p><img alt="Logistic fits to the share of Anthropic model R&amp;D tasks at or above each automation level, Aug 2025 to 2028. AL2+ and AL3+ are near 100% by mid-2026. AL4+ (AI leads or more) is fitted to reach 50% in Oct 2026. An assumed AL5 (fully automated) curve reaches 50% in Jun 2027 and 95% in Nov 2027." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/uyn1jrlwbq57i1bupkaj" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Assuming SAR comes when 95% of tasks are AL5, this predicts SAR in Nov 2027.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This method is conservative in that it doesn’t take into account the effects of automation. Of course, there is high uncertainty, especially around (a) how far AL5 will lag behind AL4 and (b) how the percentage of tasks that are AL5 relates to when SAR is achieved.</span></p><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Extrapolating software R&amp;D uplift per unit of ECI</span></h4><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ajeya Cotra has a toy model of future acceleration </span><a href="https://www.planned-obsolescence.org/p/ai-is-dramatically-accelerating-ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: If the first doubling of the pace took 12 months, and we’re now moving twice as fast, the next doubling might happen in just 6 months instead. Then moving 4x as fast, the next might take 3 months, then 1.5 months, etc., hitting limits within 12 months.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We think there’s two important corrections to this argument, which push in opposite directions. To understand why, it’s useful to have the following model in mind:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall “research effort” (i.e. research accomplished per unit time) is equal to</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[AI software R&amp;D uplift] x [exogenous inputs].</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Where exogenous inputs are some function of compute, human researchers, and data.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Every doubling of cumulative research increases ECI by k points.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-19"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-19">[19]</a></sup></span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Every ECI point causes d doublings of AI software R&amp;D uplift. (This is the same type of value that </span><a href="https://elasticity.institute/rsi-paper.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Economics of RSI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> refers to as “the increase in R&amp;D effort per additional unit of ECI”.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-20"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-20">[20]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) This has been approximately 0 for most of AI history (since AI hasn’t helped with AI R&amp;D) but now seems to be above 0.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If dk&gt;1, a doubling of cumulative research more than doubles research effort, and progress can accelerate even holding exogenous inputs fixed.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Returning to Ajeya’s model, the two concerns are:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The toy model effectively assumes that the exogenous inputs were constant, and assumes that the growth of [AI software R&amp;D uplift] was large enough to both overcome diminishing returns and to increase the rate of ECI/y by 1.7x. However, in reality, the research produced during 2026 was due to a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">combination</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of an increase in the growth rate of [AI software R&amp;D uplift] and the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">normal</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> growth of exogenous inputs. The fact that exogenous inputs still grew at their normal rate cancelled out a lot of the diminishing returns, which means that the growth rate of AI software R&amp;D uplift needed to increase somewhat less in order to achieve the 1.7x increase in ECI/y. (Relative to how much it would have had to increase without the ‘normal’ growth in exogenous inputs.) Taking this into account, we will infer lower values of dk, and dk&gt;1 is less likely.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But on the other hand, d (the number of doublings of AI software R&amp;D uplift per ECI point) must recently have been increasing, because it used to be approximately 0 (when AI didn’t help with AI R&amp;D) and now it’s non-0. And there’s a decent argument to expect d to continue increasing until full automation of AI R&amp;D, because AI is becoming more and more important for AI R&amp;D, so increasing its intelligence will be more important for productivity.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(See </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HEupcBPuQnamFn2yr?commentId=hfshPgHDHpbTFBA7B"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for a longer explanation.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Taking both these into account, my current guess is that dk is probably approaching 1. Probably a bit less right now, but d seems likely to increase and soon tip things over so that dk&gt;1 and growth compounds without further exogenous inputs. But it's not clear what time-scale this will happen on.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To nevertheless try to extrapolate, let’s try to project d into the future. I’ll assume that it will keep growing at its current slope</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-21"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-21">[21]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> until it reaches twice its current value, at which point it stays constant. I will infer d’s historical value and growth rate under the assumptions that:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI is currently increasing the pace of progress to 1.7x its historical rate.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Software efficiency has historically increased 10x/y while exogenous inputs have been growing 2x/y. This lets us infer the value of k.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-22"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-22">[22]</a></sup></span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I will make a few different assumptions about historical AI R&amp;D uplift which will be enough to constrain d’s past trajectory.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the graph below, the y-axis depicts dk, so it’s easy to see when compounding growth becomes possible.</span></p><img alt="Chart of feedback strength (dk) over time, 2026 to 2029, under five assumptions, with dk = 1 marked as the runaway threshold where research snowballs even with fixed compute and staff. A (AI accelerated the pace by 1.2x in May 2026) reaches runaway in Aug 2027; B (AI did not accelerate the pace until Nov 2025) in Sep 2027; C (dk inferred from self-reported uplift with METR's superexponential fit) in Aug 2028; D (dk inferred from METR's illustrative chart) in Sep 2028; E (METR's exponential fit) stays flat at 0.49 with no runaway. Circles mark where AI software R&amp;D uplift reaches 45x, an estimate of SAR." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/ubcmodo2mzejit9rgd6d" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this graph, the circles mark 45x AI software R&amp;D uplift as an estimate of SAR’s uplift. (The same one that would be implied by the uplift numbers in the labor uplift section above.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-23"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-23">[23]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The estimate here that’s most grounded in data is line C, which suggests SAR in Dec 2027.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-24"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-24">[24]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I’d also like to give some weight to assumptions A and B, which assume that AI only recently started significantly accelerating the pace of progress, and which suggest SAR in June and July of 2027 respectively.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s how SAR estimates from B and C vary with different AI-attributed starting accelerations:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-25"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-25">[25]</a></sup></span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A possible concern is that these models might be conservative by assuming linear growth in d, whereas we might expect growth to spike more aggressively as the fraction of automated tasks approaches 1 and everything gets unbottlenecked quickly.</span></p><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tom Davidson’s toy model</span></h4><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tom Davidson wrote up a toy model of acceleration </span><a href="https://x.com/TomDavidsonX/status/2118078519745843606"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. He describes an extremely similar trajectory to Ajeya’s original model, but his numbers don’t come from observing past progress but instead from parameters estimated in </span><a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/how-quick-and-big-would-a-software-intelligence-explosion-be#how-the-speed-of-software-progress-changes-over-time"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-26"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-26">[26]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We haven’t formed an independent impression of what the best parameter choice is (though the AIFM has an implicit view). But Tom’s bottom line being consistent with our modeling (superintelligence around a year from now, SAR presumably before then) gives us a bit more confidence.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That said, Tom says that “progress (...) may instead fizzle out” and that he was “somewhat aggressive with the numbers”, so his median may be slower.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Filtered AI Futures Model</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We developed the </span><a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Futures Model</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (AIFM) in late 2025 to forecast when important AI capability milestones will be reached. See </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/p/ai-futures-model-dec-2025-update"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for our announcement blog post, and </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/p/q25-2026-timelines-update-uplift"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for our latest update.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this section, we filter trajectories sampled from Daniel and Eli’s parameter distributions for achieving a given pace of progress acceleration above the 2024–mid-2025 rate on Nov 1. We include a condition that filters for 1.2x acceleration on May 1 in addition to 1.7x on Nov 1, given that the METR report suggested there was non-negligible AI-attributed acceleration as of 6 months ago.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-27"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-27">[27]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For Eli’s parameters, we also show a variant in which </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1ru6Okbxb6XuH18Cz8439sdQJazMV39hNxsWDokh97r0/edit?tab=t.0#heading=h.orjv6qp5erhp"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Median-to-top-human jumps above SAR needed to reach TED-AI”</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is 1.5x larger.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-28"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-28">[28]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This is based on capabilities appearing to be more spiky than we expected, with SAR potentially being imminent but other capabilities seemingly lagging behind (for example, based on the </span><a href="https://epoch.ai/benchmarks/rli?view=graph&amp;tab=release-date"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Remote Labor Index</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here are the results:</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Filtering for a single acceleration level</span></h4><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of Daniel’s sampled trajectories, 4% are at about 1.5x on Nov 1, ~1.2% are at about 1.7x, and ~1.5% are at about 2x (1.84–3x).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s a plot of acceleration level in Nov 2026 vs. p(SAR by EO2027):</span></p><img alt="Bar chart titled 'Share reaching SAR before 2028', by ECI-rate multiple on Nov 1, 2026. The share rises with the multiple: roughly 54% at ≈1.25×, 77% at ≈1.5×, 90% at ≈1.7×, 96% at ≈2× and nearly 100% at ≥3×." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/yudvlhi44vm5fnxe55nc" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">90% of 1.7x trajectories reach SAR by the end of 2027.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And here’s a plot of median SAR date by acceleration level:</span></p><img alt="Dot plot titled 'Median SAR date', by ECI-rate multiple on Nov 1, 2026. The median gets earlier as the multiple rises: from late 2027 at ≈1.25×, to about mid-2027 at ≈1.5×, early 2027 at ≈1.7×, just before the start of 2027 at ≈2×, and autumn 2026 at ≥3×." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/ophgylalklmruq52hicg" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The median SAR date for 1.7x trajectories is Feb 2027.</span></p><p><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/research-taste-linear-extrapolation"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Above</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> we discussed the importance of Sep 2026 research taste and research taste slope for forecasting the arrival of SAR.
