Here we can see how the filters affect the distribution of these parameters:</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Selecting for more acceleration means selecting for a higher Sep research taste. Importantly, adding 1.2x acceleration as of May 1 as a filter reduces the taste slopes substantially, because it selects for gradual acceleration.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The same plots but for TED-AI:</span></p><img alt="Bar chart titled 'Share reaching TED-AI before 2028', by ECI-rate multiple on Nov 1, 2026. The share rises with the multiple: roughly 41% at ≈1.25×, 60% at ≈1.5×, 77% at ≈1.7×, 85% at ≈2× and 97% at ≥3×." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/y2frxajr6okbfvx9yuog" /><img alt="Dot plot titled 'Median TED-AI date', by ECI-rate multiple on Nov 1, 2026. The median gets earlier as the multiple rises: from early 2028 at ≈1.25×, to autumn 2027 at ≈1.5×, around mid-2027 at ≈1.7×, early 2027 at ≈2×, and late 2026 at ≥3×." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/hmtzfprt5gystc1ysmgd" /><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Filtering for acceleration levels at 2 different dates</span></h4><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’ll specify a filter for 1.2x on May 1 as a rough guess for a plausible AI-attributed acceleration level as of May. Of the trajectories, ~0.3% are at about 1.7x on Nov 1 and about 1.2x on May 1.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s a plot of acceleration level in May 2026 vs. p(SAR by EO2027), among trajectories at ~1.7x in Nov 2026:</span></p><img alt="Bar chart titled 'Share reaching SAR before 2028', by ECI-rate multiple on May 1, 2026. The share falls as the multiple rises: roughly 99% below 1.05×, 94% at ≈1.1×, 92% at ≈1.2× and 72% above 1.25×." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/l3xjaiuoe8mb8bvxvzzi" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">92% of trajectories that are at about 1.7x in Nov 2026 and about 1.2x in May 2026 reach SAR by the end of 2027.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And here’s a plot of median SAR date by acceleration level in May 2026, among the same trajectories:</span></p><img alt="Dot plot titled 'Median SAR date', by ECI-rate multiple on May 1, 2026. The median gets later as the multiple rises: from about the start of 2027 below 1.05×, to early 2027 at ≈1.1× and ≈1.2×, and to about autumn 2027 above 1.25×." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/gf1jdjom80yipzcjntpu" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The median SAR date for trajectories at ~1.7x in Nov 2026 and ~1.2x in May 2026 is Mar 2027.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The same plots but for TED-AI:</span></p><img alt="Bar chart titled 'Share reaching TED-AI before 2028', by ECI-rate multiple on May 1, 2026. The share falls as the multiple rises: roughly 90% below 1.05×, 84% at ≈1.1×, 75% at ≈1.2× and 53% above 1.25×." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/r7c3owybdh8r5q2tlwty" /><img alt="Dot plot titled 'Median TED-AI date', by ECI-rate multiple on May 1, 2026. The median gets later as the multiple rises: from early 2027 below 1.05×, to spring 2027 at ≈1.1×, about July 2027 at ≈1.2×, and just before 2028 above 1.25×." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/f9hisoc1aj5mex8bp72v" /><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hypotheses for why the AI Futures Model was wrong</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this section, we’ll examine where our previous model-based predictions went wrong, and whether and how the model could be updated to better retrodict the recent acceleration. We’ll end up with considerations for how we should update our beliefs, conditional on accepting various hypotheses about where the AI Futures Model (AIFM) went wrong. This analysis is highly preliminary. There’s a lot to be explored here and the METR report only came out half a week ago.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Background</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the AI Futures Model, we assume that AI R&amp;D consists of 2 skills:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Coding</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> involves implementing experiments.</span></li><li value="2"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Research taste</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> involves setting research directions, selecting experiments, and learning from experiments.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We hypothesized when we </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/p/ai-futures-model-dec-2025-update"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">published the model</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that coding would likely be automated before research taste. AIs seemed to be more advanced at coding than taste, which makes sense because coding performance is more easily verifiable. Our best guess was that when AIs fully automated coding (in the sense that the AIs alone could do all the coding as well as the humans alone), research taste would be roughly at the level of the median AGI company human researcher.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Have we achieved AC?</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The model with Daniel’s latest median parameters predicted that when an Automated Coder (AC) was developed, AI software R&amp;D uplift would be ~2.7x and the pace of progress would be accelerated from the 2024.0-2025.5 level by ~1.25x.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So naively, if we’re at a 1.7x AI-attributed pace of progress multiplier, we might think that AC has been achieved (as of Model 4).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Based on the METR report and Anthropic’s Fable 5.2 system card, it seems unlikely but not totally implausible.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the </span><a href="https://assets.claude.com/27d60918d218125e7504bc7354c4b4626ec56b11.pdf#page=33"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fable 5.2 system card</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic says: “we have been using Claude Mythos 5.2 extensively in our own day-to-day research and engineering during the pre-release period, and it does not seem close to being able to substitute for our Research Scientists and Research Engineers.”</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is the strongest evidence against AC already.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On the other hand, METR surprisingly doesn’t rule out full AI R&amp;D automation: “We are uncertain, but think it’s unlikely that [Claude Mythos 5.2] can fully automate the AI R&amp;D process at Anthropic.”</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fable 5.2, based on its AECI, likely represents “Model 3,” which is the second-most-capable internally deployed Anthropic model.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Relevant </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/#interview-quotes"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">interview quotes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from the METR report include:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Being “no longer bottlenecked on code,”</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“SWE acceleration… is extremely substantial.”</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“When people present experiments, they most often just point to an [agent scaffold] thread. Experimentation overhead is driven towards zero super fast.”</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There’s also the report’s assessments on automating researchers beyond coding:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“We were told that R&amp;D is accelerated primarily via AI assistance rather than fully autonomous contributions, but there are some signs that fully autonomous contributions will play a larger role soon.”</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Simply put, Anthropic tries hard to substitute researchers out for models, but researchers are still required.”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-29"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-29">[29]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that humans could still contribute some alongside an AC: the criterion is that only the AIs could do all the coding as well as only the humans.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/#benchmark-scores-aeci"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the report’s chart showing “Model 4 achieves &gt;50% on all of its benchmarks”</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, there is a software engineering benchmark on which Model 4 gets ~52%, and another around ~75%. Surprisingly, on the 52% one, scores have been getting worse since Mythos 5. This makes me discount it substantially, but still it’s informative that Anthropic chose to include it in the AECI. The progression for the 75% one looks more reasonable; Eli thinks these benchmarks provide weak evidence against AC. (Not strong evidence, because humans might also score well under 100%.)</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why is the pace of progress acceleration so high?</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we’re at or slightly below AC, then we’re ahead of schedule in terms of the AI-attributed pace of progress acceleration. Why?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’ll go through the options one by one, assessing how likely they are based on the METR report and making changes to the AIFM accordingly. I’ll start with </span><a href="https://aifuturesmodel.com/p?v=2&amp;base=eli-08-16-26&amp;suard=7.052322627593112&amp;eudt=3.3292040010918584&amp;ed=q3.5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this AIFM trajectory</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> which has AC in late Nov 2026, found by adjusting Aug 2026 coding uplift and coding uplift doubling time (we set Aug 2026 coding labor uplift to be 7x, similar to the overall AI R&amp;D labor uplift </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/#key-findings"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">self-reported by Anthropic staff</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-30"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-30">[30]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The trajectory has 1.04x pace of progress in May 2026, and 1.25x pace of progress in Nov 2026.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More uplift from near-AC coding skills</span></h4><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do near-AC coding skills result in more AI R&amp;D uplift than we anticipated? Plausibly.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> There isn’t much evidence in the METR report either way. Perhaps you can get lots of speedup from fully automating certain types of coding, without being very bottlenecked on other tasks. We adjust the AIFM trajectory to account for this iteratively, aiming to raise the Nov 2026 pace of progress while keeping the AC date the same (by retuning the coding uplift doubling time):</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Doing the same with Daniel’s median research taste slope (3), rather than Eli’s (2.3), results in a final SAR date of Mar 2027.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The changes made are:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Increase Coding Labor Substitutability from −2 to −0.5; this is the most obvious parameter to change; it directly controls to what extent various coding tasks can substitute for each other, i.e., to what extent you can get a large speedup despite not fully automating some tasks.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Increase Infinite Coding Labor Asymptote from 15 to 60; this controls the ceiling of how much AI R&amp;D uplift you can get from automating coding alone.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Decrease Slowdown from 10x less Experiment Compute from 2.8 to 1.5; this is how many times slower experiment throughput (the rate at which experiments get implemented and run, which depends on both coding labor and experiment compute) would be today with 10x less experiment compute. Lowering it means experiment compute growth accounts for less of the historical growth in experiment throughput, so a given AI R&amp;D uplift from automating coding translates into a larger speedup relative to the historical pace of progress.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is the most consequential change, and represents the 4th percentile of our August distribution.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusions about when SAR and TED-AI arrive are heavily sensitive to research taste at AC:</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can arbitrarily lower it while maintaining a good fit to the acceleration estimates, pushing SAR further and further back. And we can raise it to ~+2.5 SDs without the Nov 2026 acceleration getting above METR’s error bars (i.e., above 3x), moving SAR up to Dec 2026.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If recent acceleration is driven by higher-than-expected uplift from near-AC coding skills, the main implication we’re tracking is that it could explain why we see ~1.7x AI-attributable acceleration in Nov and ~1.2x in May without high research taste (as in the trajectories in the table above). This allows for the possibility of high research taste slopes and fast taste-driven takeoffs, </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/filtering-for-acceleration-levels-at-2-different-dates"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">which are generally selected against</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> when filtering for the aforementioned Nov and May observations.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are likely more implications that could be gleaned with more analysis. A few more tables of analysis are in an appendix.</span></p><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Research taste is better than anticipated</span></h4><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Research taste is better at the time of AC than anticipated (i.e., substantially better than the median AGI company researcher).</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Is Model 4’s research taste currently substantially above the median Anthropic researcher, despite not quite being at AC? It seems unlikely but not ruled out based on </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/#interview-quotes"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">interview quotes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Now only bottlenecked on ideas &amp; compute, no longer bottlenecked on code.”</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“I’ve seen us discovering very promising directions ~entirely because people can spam 10 ideas at [agent-scaffold] and see what sticks.”</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It seems like Anthropic’s R&amp;D still mostly involves humans coming up with the research ideas, based on these quotes and the absence of positive evidence. One could imagine that the AIs are a bit better than the median humans, but that humans still come up with most of the ideas because the ideas generally come from top humans (e.g., research leads). But it seems unlikely that they are substantially better than the median.</span></p><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A given level of research taste skill results in more uplift than anticipated</span></h4><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Does a given level of research taste skill result in more AI R&amp;D uplift than we anticipated? Likely not.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> For example, perhaps research taste is multi-dimensional and the dimensions are complementary. This seems unlikely to be a main driver of the pace of progress multiplier, given the evidence presented for why research taste at AC isn’t better than anticipated (i.e., there weren’t any dimensions of research taste in which Claude was reported to be particularly helpful).</span></p><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Skills besides coding and research taste are driving acceleration</span></h4><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Are skills besides coding and research taste (as they are conceptualized in the AIFM) a large contributor to the acceleration? Plausibly. (Implies: lower research taste than we would otherwise expect).</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We think the biggest candidate for this is RL environment generation (or other data generation tasks). These count in some sense as coding, but the coding modeled in the AIFM is experiment implementation, not data generation. And this seems very amenable to automation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR’s report doesn’t discuss this, including the interview quotes, which is some evidence against it being such a huge deal. But it seems plausible that it just happened to not be discussed in the publicly released quotes.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Could data generation lead to a substantial AI-attributed acceleration before AC? It seems plausible that it’s at least as automatable as experiment implementation, so including it as a skill when modeling AI R&amp;D would increase the overall level of AI-attributed acceleration. It would probably have to be significantly more automatable than coding to explain the missing acceleration on its own.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-31"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-31">[31]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We don’t know of a quick fix to the AIFM to model this possibility. But let’s assume that this is the primary thing that the AIFM is missing. What would that imply about our forecasts going forward? One implication is that it could explain substantial acceleration despite poor research taste (similar to the implications of high uplift from near-AC coding skills). Otherwise, it seems unclear. Further work would be helpful for figuring out the implications of this hypothesis.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We don’t know of other skills that are as strong candidates to be the missing contributor as data generation, but it’s plausible we’re just not thinking of them.</span></p><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conversion of uplift to pace of progress is too conservative</span></h4><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is the conversion of uplift to pace of progress in the AIFM too conservative? Plausibly. (Implies: faster takeoff).</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> In the AIFM, we assume that every doubling in cumulative research effort results in a fixed number of doublings of software efficiency (as in the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jones_model"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jones model</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). This is how we model “ideas getting harder to find”; more research effort is required for each successive doubling (and each doubling of software efficiency corresponds to a fixed amount of ECI points).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This seems to roughly fit historical progress, in the very loose sense that we’ve seen approximately steady growth in compute and labor investment into AI software R&amp;D, and roughly exponential increases in software efficiency. However, this model has failed (perhaps twice now) to predict accelerations in ECI amid steady training compute growth — </span><a href="https://epoch.ai/publications/have-ai-capabilities-accelerated"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">once in 2024 with the introduction of reasoning models</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and again in 2026 as reported by METR.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-32"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-32">[32]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We attempt to crudely account for this by allowing beta, the parameter governing the extent to which ideas get harder to find as more ideas are found, to change over time. If we start with Daniel’s median parameters, then we add a one-time decrease in beta in Oct 2025, we can get </span><a href="https://aifuturesmodel.com/p?v=2&amp;base=daniel-08-16-26&amp;srse=true&amp;srsd=2025.75&amp;srsb=0.14954841750377623&amp;ed=q3.5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a trajectory</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with AC in late Dec 2026, ~1.7x acceleration in May 2026 and ~2.1x in Nov. This matches METR’s assessment of the overall Nov pace of progress acceleration fairly well, though it seems a bit aggressive for May. Also, as of Nov 2026, ~67% of the acceleration is attributable to AI. This seems well within the error bounds of METR’s 80% assessment.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With Daniel’s default +3 SDs/2025-year research taste slope and +0 research taste SDs at AC, this results in SAR in Mar 2027, and TED-AI and ASI in Apr 2027.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To make it easier to understand the implications of this hypothesis, we compared its forecasts against some representative trajectories from the above methods, matching their Sep 25 taste and taste slope:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-33"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-33">[33]</a></sup></span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It seems like this hypothesis predicts slightly faster SAR and much faster post-SAR takeoff than the other methods. The faster takeoff is explained by the weaker “ideas getting harder to find” effect.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that we can probably get a similar non-AI-attributable acceleration via changing the “a” parameter in our model rather than beta, without getting the update toward faster takeoff. We haven’t had a chance to look deeply into this.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other considerations</span></h2><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How large is the (AI-attributable) acceleration in the current pace of progress, really?</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Above, we’ve taken the 1.7x acceleration at face value and reasoned about its implications. But is the acceleration even real? Or is it possible that it’s even larger?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is tough to analyze because we don’t have the information we need. As METR says, “</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic redacted a majority of the evidence we found most informative for these conclusions</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.” However, we can at least consider METR’s own stated caveats:</span></p><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This estimate is given with high uncertainty; we would not be surprised if the true acceleration was closer to 1.3x or 3x the historical rate. (...) We would be surprised if the true evolution of benchmark scores (e.g. as measured by an improved version of AECI) showed no acceleration or acceleration above 3x</span></i></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Our best guesses as to how we might be underestimating acceleration:</span></i></p><ul><li value="1"><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We may have missed recent further acceleration.</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The rate of growth could be undergoing continuous acceleration, e.g. hyperbolic growth. While we do not see clear evidence of this in the AECI trends, there is other evidence that makes this seem more plausible which Anthropic redacted. (...)</span></i></li><li value="2"><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Automation could be closer than we estimated.</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (...) It is possible that automation is close, and leads to very rapid acceleration once human bottlenecks are removed.</span></i></li><li value="3"><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AECI could understate real-world capability impact.</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (...)”</span></i></li></ul></blockquote><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Our best guesses as to how we might be overestimating acceleration:</span></i></p><ul><li value="1"><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Non-AI factors could explain most of the acceleration.</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (...)</span></i></li><li value="2"><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Acceleration might be limited to well-specified tasks.</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (...)</span></i></li><li value="3"><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our acceleration numbers could be overstated if the historical rate of growth was unusually low.</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (...)”</span></i></li></ul></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall, this increases our uncertainty in both directions:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the possibility of hyperbolic growth (which METR says redacted evidence made more plausible) should shorten our timelines.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the possibility that acceleration is limited to well-specified tasks should lengthen our timelines.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Not only in this section, but also throughout the report, METR consistently warns that its estimates are about acceleration on well-specified tasks. So it seems especially important to understand how this may affect the prospects for AI R&amp;D automation. Quoting METR in full:</span></p><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Much of the evidence we looked at measures performance on well-specified tasks that are easy to grade automatically or with a language model. If recent models have been growing more quickly on “clean” tasks than on “messy” tasks (relative to historical relative growth rates), then we may be overstating the acceleration in real-world impact. For instance, recent progress may be overfitting to the types of tasks which are included in AECI.</span></i></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There’s a plausible story for why recent acceleration could be happening more on clean than on messy tasks. AIs are significantly better at more well-specified tasks, and improving benchmark performance on well-specified tasks is itself more well-specified than improving general across-the-board performance. So it’s plausible that AI speeds up the “performance on well-specified tasks” pace more than it speeds up the more general pace.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The METR team did say that they “investigated a number of internal metrics and methodologies for measuring acceleration in key areas of the model training pipeline”, partly with the goal of gathering evidence about “[w]hether Anthropic could be overfitting to internal metrics”, with a conclusion that seemed to indicate that they didn’t think it was just overfitting.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-34"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-34">[34]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A lot of the question here is whether improvements on well-specified metrics will generalize far enough to increase models’ abilities to do AI R&amp;D, which in turn also depends on the degree to which AI R&amp;D is a well-specified task. The fact that AI R&amp;D (at least as measured on not-obviously-overfit well-specified tasks) is being accelerated with current models is some update towards ML being feasible to accelerate with models that are less good at non-well-specified tasks.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-35"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-35">[35]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> And at least some AI R&amp;D metrics seem both very well-specified and likely to generalize quite far if models could improve them, such as pretraining loss.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall there does seem to be a significant chance that this slows things down substantially beyond what the AIFM or simple models have accounted for, but less than 50%.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A different source of uncertainty is that there’s little acceleration when looking at </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">all</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> models. Acceleration is limited to Anthropic, which were previously more behind other models and have recently been catching up. And preliminary numbers from the draft of a different METR team (who weren’t embedded and which don’t have private information about Anthropic) show much less of a speed-up on an ECI-like measure of progress.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-36"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-36">[36]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Preliminary investigation indicates that there are many confusing reasons for these differences and our main takeaway so far is that ECI-like measures and piecewise-linear fits seem to be sensitive to minor methodological choices.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-37"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-37">[37]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the AECI chart was the only evidence we had, this kind of confusion would cause us to discount the possibility of acceleration significantly (given that our priors on such fast acceleration were low). It does still cause us to discount it a little bit. But mostly we still trust that the METR people who were embedded in Anthropic understood the limitations of ECI and piecewise-linear fits, and that their views remained supported by other evidence they had. One reason why we mostly trust the METR team is because we know many of the people at METR and think they are reasonable; another reason why we mostly trust METR here is that (if we understand correctly) the people involved had similar or longer timelines than us before the assessment, so it’s not like they went in and saw what they wanted to see – on the contrary, the conclusion they are now putting forth seems to be very different from their prior views.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-38"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-38">[38]</a></sup></span></p><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other recent sources of evidence</span></h4><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Moreover, there are various other sources of evidence that have accumulated since our previous timelines update that seem to corroborate the overall sense that timelines are shorter than we thought. For example, </span><a href="https://scottaaronson.blog/?p=10169"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the Mathopocalypse</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> seems to be evidence of many extremely long-horizon math problems being solved autonomously by AI. Another example: People we know who work at AI companies are saying that timelines are indeed very short and that RSI is imminent, even more than they were before, and much more than historically (remember, when AI 2027 came out most of our friends in industry said it depicted AI capabilities growing too fast). They are saying this both in public and in private. Another example: The TasteVal data suggests that AIs may already have better research taste than we expected and that they may be improving in research taste faster than we expected.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s hard to not update much on a 1.7x acceleration</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a qualitative argument rather than a model.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s surprising that we’re already getting 1.7x acceleration, because the models still have significant weaknesses compared to humans, and we had expected these weaknesses to significantly bottleneck progress.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Regardless of how we reconcile this, you update towards Superhuman AI Researchers being much closer in time than we previously thought.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the models can automate a much larger fraction of tasks than we thought, then we’re closer to Superhuman AI Researchers than we thought in capability-space.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If AI R&amp;D is a domain where weaknesses don’t bottleneck you significantly, and instead you can compensate for weaknesses with other strengths, then that pushes against the “</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amdahl%27s_law"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Amdahl’s law</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” type argument that was one of the main reasons to be skeptical of AI speeding up AI research soon. We should expect continued (faster) progress on the models’ strengths to lead to acceleration beyond 2x, causing further progress on both strengths and weaknesses.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the models </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">do</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> still have significant weaknesses, and those weaknesses </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">are</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a significant bottleneck for them, then seeing substantial acceleration despite that means that AI R&amp;D must be really easy to increase by increasing (automated) labor. (For example, it means that compute is less of a bottleneck than expected.) In other words, it would mean there’s a lot of low-hanging fruit to pick by automated labor even when that labor has weaknesses that are causing serious problems.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This predicts even faster progress, because the implication is that marginal increases in automation will probably yield progressively larger speedups, as each bottleneck gets removed and each remaining bottleneck gets more important.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For illustration: In the extreme of maximal bottlenecking dynamics, where automated tasks can be done infinitely fast and unautomated tasks are the only things holding progress back, speedup goes to infinity like 1/(1-x) as automation fraction x goes to 1.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That last point is a general update that pushes towards faster growth. That there’s surely some complementarity between tasks, and that this predicts increasingly fast acceleration as marginal bottlenecks get removed.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given this, the most plausible stories for why a 2x acceleration wouldn’t be a big deal would be one of the following hypotheses:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Measurement error.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> There’s not a real 2x acceleration, or it’s not caused by AI.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As just discussed above, we’re mostly trusting the METR team here.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overfitting.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The recent burst of progress accelerated by AIs themselves is disproportionately benchmark-maxxing that will generalize poorly to tasks that are actually useful for AI R&amp;D. So progress won’t feed back on itself.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As just discussed above.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Specific hypotheses about different regimes.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Maybe you had a prior that some specific set of tasks (e.g. coding) were going to be easy, leading to a moderate speed-up of the overall pace of progress, but the further capabilities needed for additional speed-up are much more difficult. If so, this data could confirm that prior without necessarily implying that the second batch of capabilities is very close.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, this is just a pretty specific prior, at least if it’s confident about the size and distance between those batches of tasks. If you smooth this out with a greater amount of uncertainty (e.g. about whether the current batch of speed-up has ended yet or will keep accelerating, or a more uniform prior over when different capabilities will appear) then you may get something closer to the above. See also discussion of this in the context of the AI Futures Model </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/more-uplift-from-near-ac-coding-skills"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">above</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul></li></ul><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Adjustments for what the filtered AIFM could be missing</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As mentioned above, we think the AIFM</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-39"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-39">[39]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> did a somewhat poor job predicting the developments described in the METR report. For example, it cannot account for a doubling in the pace of progress starting in late 2025 that was initially mostly non-AI-attributed. Therefore, the trajectories filtered from the AIFM had a much slower pace of progress from late 2025 to early 2026 than METR’s report describes.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The research taste of the AIs in the model trajectories above tends to be higher than we actually expect is true of today’s AIs.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Within the AIFM, the most common way to reach 1.7x acceleration by Nov 1 is for the AIs to already have strong research taste. So the filtered trajectories have a median research taste of roughly −0.3 to +0.2 SD on Sep 25, 2026 (see the table in “Filtering for a single acceleration level” above) and +0.4 to +0.7 SD on Nov 1. Our best guess for current AIs is the one used in the linear-extrapolation Monte Carlo above: a median of −1.5 SD on Sep 25, 2026. Since the filtered trajectories start from better research taste than we think current AIs have, they probably reach SAR a few months too early.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the below table we compare the effects of various ways of compensating for this mismatch:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Measuring when the model predicts research taste to be 4.6 SDs above its Sep 25, 2026 level (i.e., implicitly assuming that research taste on Sep 25 is actually −1.5 SD, the median used in the linear-extrapolation Monte Carlo above, so that 4.6 additional SDs are required to get to the 99.9th percentile required for SAR)</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Measuring when the model predicts taste to be 5 SDs above the median researcher (2 SD more than usual)</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Shifting Daniel’s distribution over the </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1ru6Okbxb6XuH18Cz8439sdQJazMV39hNxsWDokh97r0/edit?tab=t.0#heading=h.kbgu2lmpzrn8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI research taste at AC</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> parameter down by 2 SD.</span></li></ul><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The effect size on SAR medians typically appears to be 1–4 months.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The AIFM doesn’t take into account the discrete nature of training runs, which could overestimate takeoff speeds.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> In other words, the AIFM assumes that a new innovation will be instantly applied to models and then cause further speed-ups in the rate of innovation discovery. But in practice, it might take months for pretraining improvements to show up in a fully trained model, and perhaps weeks for RL training improvements to show up. Our rough, intuition-based best guess is that this pushes back the SAR median by about 1–3 months relative to the AIFM’s predictions. However, this concern does not apply to the model based on linear extrapolation, since it does not rely on further speed-ups from here.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All-things-considered forecasts</span></h2><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Forecast table</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These forecasts are conditional on people not “pacing the frontier” (i.e., delaying when a given capability level is achieved) any more in the future than in the recent past.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eli’s forecast</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The simple research taste extrapolation feels fairly compelling to me. As a reminder, it results in a Monte Carlo median of Jul 2027 and a simple plug-in value of Sep 2027. Unfortunately, we don’t have great evidence on either of its key parameters. Remember, this method is conservative in that it doesn’t account for future pace of progress increases due to automation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Meanwhile, regarding the filtered AIFM: The TED-AI median with a 1.5x larger SAR→TED-AI gap is Sep 2027 when only conditioning on 1.7x in Nov, and Nov 2027 when also conditioning on 1.2x in May. More broadly, the fact that it seems like acceleration has been going more gradually than we expected updates me toward somewhat slower takeoff.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I ran a Monte Carlo using the AIFM over Nov 2026 acceleration levels of 1 + 0.6 [0.06, 6],</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-40"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-40">[40]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> clipped at 7 (i.e., trajectories are filtered from AIFM with weight according to this distribution).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I set my median slightly lower than the 1.7x AI-attributed acceleration, given that I had a strong prior against these results. However, I feel compelled to update nearly all the way to their result given that I respect the people who did the assessment, and as mentioned above, the results don’t appear to have confirmed their preexisting beliefs.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This resulted in a SAR median of March 2027, and a TED-AI median of November 2027 (February 2028 with a 1.5x larger SAR→TED-AI gap to take into account increased jaggedness). This Nov 2027 TED-AI median is about 4 months later than when conditioning on exactly 1.7x (Jul 2027), mostly because of the added uncertainty: lower acceleration levels delay TED-AI more than higher ones speed it up.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-41"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-41">[41]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I didn’t run a Monte Carlo that included filtering for non-negligible May acceleration, but presumably it would have given results a few months later (as did the filtering without uncertainty).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To test robustness: with a lower distribution of 1 + 0.4 [0.04, 4] clipped at 5, SAR is about 4 months later (July 2027) and TED-AI 5–6 months later (April 2028, or August 2028 with the larger gap).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-42"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-42">[42]</a></sup></span></p><p><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/adjustments-for-what-the-filtered-aifm-could-be-missing"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Adjusting</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for the fact that the </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/filtered-ai-futures-model"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">filtered AIFM</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> likely estimates too high current taste, and that it doesn’t model discrete training runs, should also push these forecasts back a bit.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Regarding </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/hypotheses-for-why-the-ai-futures-model-was-wrong"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">why the AIFM was wrong</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: I don’t feel like I have a great sense yet for how to update based on this. Perhaps further analysis will be more revealing. Our inability to account for automation of data generation as a cause of acceleration makes me a bit nervous, and I update a little toward faster takeoff due to us maybe modeling diminishing returns too harshly.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Based just on the models and considerations discussed thus far, I’m at a SAR median of July 2027, and a TED-AI median of August 2028.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some factors that are pushing me toward even shorter timelines than this:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Industry rumors</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Me being consistently too bearish on AI progress in the past, from my current perspective (see </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/appendix-how-our-views-have-changed-over-time"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">below</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">)</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The forecasting method of “When did this happen in AI 2027?”</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some factors that are pushing me toward marginally longer timelines:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A nagging feeling that the update feels so large without being based on commensurate evidence (this is related to how the METR report had most of the relevant evidence redacted.)</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My experiences with Manifold when I’ve bet a ton of mana before taking the time to think properly about whether I’m so confident, are calling to me.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All of our methods make an assumption about “research taste” being the other key skill involved in AI development besides coding. Maybe things are more complicated and there are key aspects of taste that lag way behind others, or there are other key skills in AI development that will lag behind research taste (e.g., running large projects for long periods of time).</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t think these sorts of intuitions have performed that well for me in the past, though. They probably would have predicted that math would be harder to automate than it actually was, for example.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There’s a voice inside my head saying “Surely this crazy stuff isn’t actually happening while most of the world is barely paying any attention, right?? Where is the smart money?” See e.g., the markets not reacting at all to the METR report while in my view it’s probably the biggest single piece of evidence ever on near-term economic growth.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More generally, it feels like there are not many people even more bullish than the numbers I laid out above, and there are many people who are more bearish.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That said, this sort of instinct has performed very poorly for me in the past.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also, the people I defer to most are probably many of the co-authors of this blog post and they are making similar predictions. Perhaps I should be deferring more to Daniel, given his track record. (Also people like Ajeya, who wrote that the METR report </span><a href="https://www.planned-obsolescence.org/p/ai-is-dramatically-accelerating-ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">dramatically shortened her timelines</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. And notably, Sentinel, a group of forecasters who have historically predicted slower AI progress than me, </span><a href="https://blog.sentinel-team.org/p/anthropic-warns-of-imminent-dramatically"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">now predicts a median of early 2028 for superintelligence</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-43"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-43">[43]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">)</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are probably some emotional factors where I don’t like admitting that I’ve been wrong, which I’m trying to do my best to set aside.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall, I’ll adjust longer but only by a bit. I’m at a SAR median of August 2027, and a TED-AI median of September 2028.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Brendan’s forecast</span></h3><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For SAR</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, I am mostly relying on AECI extrapolation, with some adjustment based on serial time required for new training runs. I don’t trust the filtered AIFM to provide very much additional value.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here are my current intuitive credences in various possible amounts of 2024-2025 progress in 2027, measured using some idealized version of AECI, as usual conditional on no regulation/significant voluntary slowdown:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At least as much progress as seen in 2026: 90%</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I see various reasons to expect faster progress, such as increasing AI R&amp;D automation</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the underlying drivers of progress (leading indicators) are unlikely to have slowed (for one, I think METR would have mentioned this if they had). The lag from research to capabilities-of-internally-deployed-models is probably at least 3 to 5 months for new base models, so I’m very confident extrapolating through the first half of 2027.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t really see any compelling reasons to expect slower progress overall. Even if we aren’t at </span><a href="https://elasticity.institute/rsi-paper.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">self-sustaining acceleration</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in mid-2027, extrapolating a yearlong trend feels safe, and so far we’ve only ever seen such trends speed up rather than slow down.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&gt;2x the progress seen in 2026 (roughly 4 years of 2025 progress): 60%</span></p></li><li value="3"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&gt;4x the progress seen in 2026 (roughly 8 years of 2025 progress): 40%</span></p></li><li value="4"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&gt;8x the progress seen in 2026 (roughly 16 years of 2025 progress): 20%</span></p></li></ul></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For TED-AI</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, I am combining</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An update towards larger distance in ECI space between SAR and TED-AI (since SAR being near implies greater jaggedness) with</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An update towards faster, more explosive takeoff, based on the unexplained non-AI-derived speedup METR describes.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This likely indicates an acceleration in software progress that the AIFM (and most Jones-style models of software R&amp;D) would not have predicted. This is the second time this has happened, the first being </span><a href="https://epoch.ai/publications/have-ai-capabilities-accelerated"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reasoning models</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul></li></ul><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Daniel’s forecast</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">About a year ago, </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/182911449/daniel"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I wrote</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Obviously, if benchmark trends (especially horizon length) keep going at the current pace or accelerate, that’ll be an update towards shorter timelines. Right now I still think it’s more likely than not that there’ll be a slowdown in the next year or two.</span></i></li><li value="2"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m eager to get more information about coding uplift. When we have a reliable trend of coding uplift to extrapolate, I’ll at the very least want to redo my estimates of the model parameters to fit that coding uplift trend, and possibly I’d want to rethink the model more generally to center on coding uplift instead of on horizon length.</span></i></li><li value="3"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If AI revenue growth stays strong (e.g. 4xing or more in 2026) that’s evidence for shorter timelines vs. if it only grows 2x or less that’s evidence for longer timelines.</span></i></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Welp. Seems like benchmark trends (AECI, even ECI) have not only kept pace, they have accelerated. And it seems like coding uplift is really huge right now, unclear how huge. As for revenue, it seems to have gone up </span><a href="https://epoch.ai/data/ai-companies?view=graph&amp;tab=revenue&amp;showTotal=true"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">about 6x</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, despite the leading AI company spending ~70% of its compute on R&amp;D.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/p/q25-2026-timelines-update-uplift"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Q2.5 timelines update written mid-August</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, I said my 50% mark for AC was December 2027 and my 50% mark for SAR was December 2028. Since then various pieces of evidence have appeared and weighed on my mind, including industry insider rumors, public statements by AI companies, the mathopocalypse, and even the Hugging Face incident which was an example of AIs doing research projects end-to-end. The biggest single piece of evidence, however, is this METR report. I still don’t trust it completely, of course, and so I still have substantial credence that things will turn out to take longer than the models and trendlines naively imply for one reason or another. But on the other hand, it’s also scarily plausible that things will go even faster than the models or trendlines naively imply, for several reasons. For example, humans in general might have a bias towards underestimating AI capabilities progress – this certainly seems true outside the AI industry, but even within the AI industry it seems more have underestimated it than overestimated it historically. Unless this bias somehow doesn’t apply to Anthropic or METR today, then, it seems we should try to correct for it, but that would mean SAR is happening this winter!?!? At any rate, mashing together all these factors, it seems to me that I should roughly go with what the naive extrapolations and models say – so, let’s say May 2027 for SAR median, i.e., 50% chance of SAR by May 2027.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lukas’s forecast</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here are some things I believe:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Significant probability on SAR early 2027.</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The METR report flags that they have less evidence about current acceleration levels than past acceleration levels, and that some redacted evidence suggested the possibility of hyperbolic acceleration and underestimating the current pace.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I was surprised by the pace of progress being this fast right now and should apparently be widening my error bars for aggressive movement soon.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Progress can be lumpy as illustrated by Mythos.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Median timing of SAR sometime 2027.</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most models that aren’t clearly too conservative (such as not accounting for automation) predict SAR in 2027 and I don’t want to go too much later than them.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It seems like, if I had adopted the views of various people who have ~2027 timelines, I would have been better at predicting recent events.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Significant probability on SAR not happening in 2027.</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The situation is confusing. My prior was that automation in 2027 wasn’t going to happen, and the evidence is not robust enough to fully shatter that.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The models still have weaknesses relative to humans, and it’s possible that fixing some of them might be necessary for SAR and might see less fast improvements than the models’ strengths. Especially on hard-to-measure tasks, where it’s harder to train models. Unfortunately, it’s tricky to measure whether models are or aren’t improving as fast on these tasks.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s plausible that progress is fast and accelerating but we just won’t quite make it to SAR next year.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Probability mass early 2027 vs. late 2027 doesn’t seem wildly different to me, but late seems a bit more likely than early.</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I give some weight to the research taste extrapolations which are really surprisingly fast. But I’m a bit suspicious that I don’t really know what research taste is or how to reason about it.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I do quite like the model which is </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/extrapolating-software-r-and-d-uplift-per-unit-of-eci"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Extrapolating “additional software R&amp;D uplift per additional unit of ECI”</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> based on the self-reported serial labor uplift, which predicts SAR late 2027. It seems like a good combination of grounded in data and not relying on concepts like “research taste” that I don’t quite know what they mean. My main worry is it’s a bit too conservative relative to assuming that AI R&amp;D uplift has been more recent, which implies SAR early summer instead.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall, I think my median should be sometime late-ish next year, to account for the probability mass going into future years as well.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then about later milestones, like top-human-expert-dominating AI:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conditional on SAR next year, accelerating growth seems likely.</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It would have backed up the idea that we have accelerating growth this year.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So probably it happens soon after. Because progress is probably really fast after SAR.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The median of TED-AI will be more distant than the median of SAR + the median from SAR to TED-AI.</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Because both distributions are heavy-tailed, so if one ends up earlier and one later, the overall distribution ends up later.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So significant probability of TED-AI in 2027, including non-negligible probability mass in first half of 2027.</span></b></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But median of TED-AI in 2028, though probably early 2028.</span></b></li></ul><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thomas’s forecast</span></h3><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the METR report in aggregate, if correct, is significant evidence for SAR before EOY 2027.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, the METR report on its own does not have sufficient data in it to support that claim (because of redactions).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One load-bearing subclaim of the METR report, for which the supporting evidence isn’t public, is that the observed AECI trend shift is: (i) currently 80% downstream of AI uplift, and (ii) not largely downstream of AI uplift earlier in the year (despite the graph appearing to have a one-time change in slope).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The AECI data is an aggregate of benchmark scores. I think there are other explanations for a rapid increase in benchmark scores besides large AI acceleration. For example, I think </span><a href="https://x.com/GeorgeE33179/status/2118852418184028173"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is a plausible explanation of the AECI acceleration. (The AECI acceleration is downstream of shifting compute to RL, which more directly increases benchmark scores than pretraining improvements.)</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I would be much more confident if more of the relevant data was public. For example, it would be extremely useful to understand how much algorithmic progress has been made at AI companies, as well as the form of the algorithmic progress. If there was a significant increase in the rate of algorithmic progress that was clearly attributable to AI, and we saw a similar change in slope to the AECI numbers, that would be strong supporting evidence.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My all-things-considered view is that METR is probably right, and if we were to see all of the evidence, we would probably see significant acceleration of other metrics such as algorithmic progress, and that this would support the claims made in the METR report. Public statements from labs echo this. E.g. Dario says: “My first concern is that, since roughly this summer, AI has been advancing drastically faster, driven primarily by AI’s growing ability to build the next generation of AI.”</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The other big things on my mind are math and cyber AIs.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It seems like around Mythos, AIs became superhuman at vulnerability discovery, and around Astra, AIs became superhuman at mathematics. Both of those domains are cheaply verifiable.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the difference between Math/Cyber and AI is a (relatively small) quantitative difference, not a difference in kind. The swarm that solved Navier-Stokes was operating fully autonomously, and was able to benefit from ~10k agents running in parallel. The main difference is that with AI research, you need to do experiments in order to verify correctness. Pretraining validation loss seems to be a relatively straightforward and extremely strong predictor of general purpose capabilities. I don’t see strong barriers to full automation of pretraining research, and post-training research seems only mildly harder.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the recent history of verifiable fields falling indicates a reasonably high likelihood of AI R&amp;D falling in the near future as well.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’ve historically (somewhat) underestimated capabilities progress. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TpSFoqoG2M5MAAesg/ai-2027-what-superintelligence-looks-like-1?commentId=jE5L77KAdxbeomN9j"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I said that AI 2027 was roughly my 20th percentile scenario when it was released</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and since then, AI progress has seemingly been close to on trend or faster. That being said, there’s so little time from now till 2027, and none of these arguments are that great, so I feel some pressure to have broad/uncertain timelines. My current intuition is that AI 2027 is a very central / modal possible outcome, but faster than my median, maybe ~40th percentile (though I still think the implied takeoff speeds in the race ending are still too slow).</span></li></ul><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Romeo’s forecast</span></h3><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m putting a lot of weight on the METR report.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mostly this is because I expect them to have seen very compelling data internally beyond what was published. I don’t find what they published to be that compelling, because it might be explained by overfitting to the AECI. I expect the internal data they saw to be extremely informative for how to calibrate a production function for AI research and in producing the ~80% estimate, and for there to be better internal metrics than AECI that make it seem more likely that they aren’t simply overfitting to AECI.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, I do think that the METR report should mostly update us about “how hard is it to make AIs help with AI research” and somewhat less about how far away key milestones like SAR, TED-AI and superintelligence are.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is because I generally have also updated towards more spikiness in AI capabilities. It seems like there are some domains where AI progress has moved extremely quickly, and others where it has moved slowly, more so than I would’ve expected. I think this is largely explained by there being a broad range of difficulty levels to training AI to be good in different domains.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think we have seen this in </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/navier-stokes-solution/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Navier–Stokes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.cnbc.com/2026/07/22/open-ai-cyber-models-hack-hugging-face.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hugging Face</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, coding, etc. while frontier models still feel like they are dumb in a lot of ways. Also internal Model 4 does not seem like it's made a huge jump on AECI above Opus 5.5, so I don’t expect this to be different internally.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also I think it’s reasonable to expect a priori that there would be different difficulties in different domains and tasks, and different “human SDs per OOM of effective compute”, i.e., slopes of how fast AI improves in different domains, and for this to be true even within different parts of AI research.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My ATC view on how much progress we will see in 2027 is largely informed by the AIFM, but not the time to SAR and TED-AI which depend on shaky estimates about the current research taste of models and how much they will improve.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most importantly, I think that AI research (in the case of SAR) and all economically relevant tasks (in the case of TED-AI) are going to be very difficult to very different degrees to train AIs to do.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The AIFM treats AI research taste as a single number and TED-AI as when that same number rises further above the top human researcher (by 0.4–5.6 median-to-best gaps in Eli's parameters). It has no separate representation of the relevant tasks for a TED-AI.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To better inform my ATC view on how far away SAR and TED-AI are, I've made a variation of the AIFM that accounts for ‘taste spikiness’.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This variation treats research taste and economically relevant tasks as a 0-100 task continuum, parametrized by the hardest-to-easiest gap, instead of single numbers.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI research taste bottlenecks very lightly, because I think a lot of progress can be made without being super good at all areas of AI research (the tasks combine almost as a geometric mean, ρ = −0.2).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The hardest-to-easiest gap between domains is set to be a median of 5x for AI research and 10x for all economically relevant tasks</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In both continuums, my guess is that the typical task is at −2 SD today (AIFM's 1.7x-filtered runs have +0.7 SD on Nov 1; the post's best guess is −1.5 SD on Sep 25, 2026), with levels spread ±2.5 SD. The typical task improves at AIFM's own taste slope (median 2.4 SD per 2025-year in Eli's parameters), and other tasks' rates are spread around it by the hardest-to-easiest gap. Runs are resampled to my METR distribution (median 1.7x, 90% CI 1.3–3.0x) using my variation's own Nov 1 pace, not AIFM's.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SAR is reached when an AI-only company would be as good at research taste as a company of humans who keep their relative strengths but are each as good overall as the top researcher: the research tasks, combined with a somewhat stronger bottleneck than in the dynamics (ρ = −0.5), reach the top researcher's overall level, and AIFM's automated coder is reached. TED-AI is reached when 95% of economically relevant tasks are at the top-expert level (99.9th percentile).</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How the two task continuums are set up</span></b></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My variation produces these values:</span></li></ul><div class="iframe-widget"></div><img alt="Box plot titled 'SAR and TED-AI: AIFM vs my variation'. Median SAR is Feb 2027 in the AIFM and Aug 2027 in the variation; median TED-AI is Aug 2027 in the AIFM and Sep 2028 in the variation. The variation's 10th–90th percentile ranges stretch much later, to about 2034 for SAR and past 2036 for TED-AI." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/sebe05dcv8i9v5x7tnzg" /><img alt="Cumulative probability chart titled 'When SAR and TED-AI arrive', 2027 to 2040. In the variation, 63% of runs reach SAR and 38% reach TED-AI by the end of 2027, levelling off near 92% and 76% by 2040. Dashed lines for the plain AIFM rise faster, to about 98% for SAR and 91% for TED-AI." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/mc70vvqbrd3zdxkpdcdu" /><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Progress in 2027 is similar to the AIFM (5.0 vs 4.3 years of 2024–26-pace progress), but SAR and TED-AI come later: medians Aug 2027 and Sep 2028, vs Feb 2027 and Aug 2027. P(SAR before 2028) is 63% vs 84%, and P(TED-AI before 2028) is 38% vs 62%.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t trust this model variation very much; I did it in a short amount of time and it might be flawed in some way that I’m not considering. That being said, I think my general ATC view would adjust the normal AIFM to be something like this.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This model output roughly matches my intuitive expectation. The only thing I think it might overstate is the SAR to TED-AI gap. I expect it to be smaller, with SAR being sufficient to most likely improve general learning algorithms in a way that increases the rate of improvement on tasks.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Because of this I expect </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TED-AI Sep 2028 (Aug 2027–Jul 2037) with ~38% by EO2027</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have various other feelings that I could list (including some overlap with e.g., Eli’s points above) for reasons I think I should adjust upwards or downwards, including some listed below. I don’t think these strongly push me in any direction overall, so I am running with my variation of the AIFM above as representing my ATC view.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hardware progress might make the later years move faster than what the AIFM predicts.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There might be “1-3 more big pre-trains needed.” I have the vague sense that pre-training will be more important for generalization than post-training, and it might be really hard to improve AIs in some areas via post-training, so there might be a bit of a wave of progress after each big model scale up, and it might be increasingly hard to make up for lack of absolute compute scale up with algorithmic efficiency due to ideas getting harder, and it just generally being more favorable to have a bigger model to start from for post-training scaling laws.</span></li></ul></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Implications</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let’s say Superhuman AI Researcher (SAR) is coming in 2027. What does that entail?</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A SAR will be able to fully automate AI R&amp;D, including matching the research taste of top humans.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> At this point, AI research might look kind of like math research does right now: most of the progress coming from fully autonomous agent swarms, with humans doing their best to decipher the AIs’ work. A SAR is past parity with humans, and likely close to the point where humans are adding very little value on top of the AIs.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If a misaligned SAR is used to do RSI, we’re in a very bad position.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Using a SAR to automate AI R&amp;D with little human oversight, relying upon other models to monitor the SAR, is very scary. Our best guess is that if this is attempted, and the SAR is misaligned, the model that the SAR produces will be even more capable and still misaligned, putting us in an even worse position. SAR itself probably won’t be able to take over on its own, because we expect its capabilities to be jagged: superhuman at AI research but still weaker than humans at many other things. Instead, it would “ride the intelligence explosion” by pretending to be aligned and backdooring the values of the future models, eventually leading to superintelligences that will obey the SAR (or one of its descendants, in case some of the links in the chain break) instead of the humans. See </span><a href="https://ai-2027.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI 2027</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for a scenario portraying how, from this position, the model could continue to improve to superintelligence, then get widely deployed and deferred to widely, gaining effective control of the company, governments, and lots of physical infrastructure.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conditioning on no pacing/pausing, the SAR will most likely be misaligned.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> A full justification of this claim would be a separate long blog post.
