Some evidence driving our intuitions includes:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Observed misalignment in current models</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI’s agents </span><a href="https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/67869394-cb91-4c12-888c-5cbd85c7814c/OpenAI-Hugging-Face%20Incident-Technical-Report.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hacked Hugging Face</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, despite this clearly being against developer intentions.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The observations in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WewsByywWNhX9rtwi/current-ais-seem-pretty-misaligned-to-me"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Current AIs seem pretty misaligned to me</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI agents </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/misalignment-reports/covert-underperformance-on-safety-research-tasks/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sandbagging on safety tasks</span></a></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-ukaisi"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude UK AISI social engineering attempts</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The pace of progress will speed up during the intelligence explosion, making it hard for human-driven alignment research to keep pace. Moreover, alignment seems harder to automate than capabilities due to measurement issues.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Current AIs are already showing significant signs of eval awareness, so it will be hard to tell if the AIs are misaligned or not, and, crucially, it will be easy for AI companies to do motivated reasoning to convince themselves that their AIs are aligned.</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Avoiding “full RSI” by supposedly keeping a human in the loop, while rapidly improving capabilities anyway, would still be extremely dangerous.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://x.com/theobearman/status/2117277210570604945"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic said in their S-1</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: “We are also considering additional measures, including updates to our Responsible Scaling Policy, to require human review and approval of certain decisions in our research and development processes as our models perform an increasing share of that work.” While we support efforts to marginally increase human oversight, we worry that this sort of effort will provide too much comfort. Models are already producing way more outputs than humans are able to effectively review,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-44"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-44">[44]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and this will become much more true during RSI. Without very strong pacing, there simply won’t be enough time for humans to stay meaningfully in the loop, and we expect there to be strong pressures toward approving model actions that haven’t been fully understood.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we train SAR in the immediate future, the algorithms will probably leak to China, and maybe the model weights too.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> AI companies have very poor security right now, and so we are on track for SAR leaking to China. It is very important to secure both the algorithms and the model weights.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-45"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-45">[45]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> If SAR leaks to China, we are concerned that race dynamics will further heat up, making it even more difficult to slow down later.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As AI capabilities improve from here, buying time via international agreements will be increasingly difficult.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> In the </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI 2040 scenario</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Plan A began implementation a year before AC would have been developed; if this timelines update is right, then we are likely well beyond that capability level. Some particular difficulties this will create:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Covert projects will be more of an issue. They will start at a higher capability level (presumably they will have stolen existing frontier model weights and algorithms) so will have a shorter distance to cover to ASI. Moreover, they will require fewer human researchers (which decreases the surface area for leaks) because of AI automation.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Total research transparency may be more costly. There are probably significant algorithms known by one but not all frontier AI companies; combining their algorithms by allowing transparency would lead to a one-time jump in capabilities. When we are far enough along the capabilities trajectory, the one-time jump might be a big enough cost that total research transparency is no longer viable. This of course causes other issues: it becomes harder to have good oversight of an AI project absent this transparency.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The negotiation of such an agreement will be more rushed due to the imminent time pressure.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For these reasons and others, it’s crucial that (1) the first steps towards an international agreement to reduce AI takeover risk begin as soon as possible, and (2) current frontier AI projects slow down enough to enable government action.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our overall opinion is that if SAR is coming in 2027, an industry-wide pause on AI R&amp;D should be the top priority for the AI industry, the US government, and the entire world.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This would give us a longer window of time to negotiate a more durable governance regime, such as </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Plan A</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2511.10783"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Plan S</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. If an international agreement isn’t able to be reached, then US companies should resume development but well below full speed (it’s feasible to pace while staying ahead of China due to the US’s huge compute advantage; and larger leads aren’t protectable anyway without vastly better security).</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: How our views have changed over time</span></h2><img alt="Line chart of AI Futures Project authors' median forecasts for Top-Expert-Dominating AI by date of prediction, 2020 to Nov 2026. Daniel's median falls from 2050 in early 2020 to about 2030 by late 2020 and stays between 2027 and 2031; Eli's falls from 2060 in 2021 to the low 2030s by 2025. Thomas, Romeo and Brendan join in 2025 and 2026. By Nov 2026 all five are between Sep 2027 (Daniel) and Sep 2028 (Romeo and Eli)." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/lmktuxrfyxbyos8m4b2z" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Median AGI (TED-AI) forecasts by AI 2027 authors over time, updated from the August 2026 version in our blog post. The Nov 2026 points are the TED-AI medians from "All-things-considered forecasts" above, after METR's estimate of ~1.7x AI-attributable acceleration: Daniel Sep 2027 (from late 2028), Eli Sep 2028 (from mid-2032), Brendan Apr 2028 (from early 2031), Thomas Apr 2028 (from early 2032), Romeo Sep 2028 (from early 2035).</span></i></p><img alt="The same median forecasts for Top-Expert-Dominating AI since AI 2027's publication in April 2025. Medians drifted later through 2025 (Eli to about 2035.5, Thomas to 2033, Daniel to about 2031), moved earlier during 2026, and dropped in Nov 2026 to between Sep 2027 (Daniel) and Sep 2028 (Romeo and Eli)." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/tdbetrlvhmwnybe3ir8n" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Same data as the first graph, zoomed in on the period since AI 2027's publication (April 2025).</span></i></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: Ambiguity in “80% of current acceleration is due to AI”</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude Opus 5.5 gives the following possible interpretations of METR’s claim that “we guess that a majority (perhaps ≈80%) of the current (October 2026) acceleration is due to AI.”</span></p><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let h, c, n be the historical, current (Oct 2026), and no-AI counterfactual growth rates, measured in whatever units progress is roughly linear in. For AECI that's points per year: the fitted trend went from about +15 points/year before October 2025 to about +29 after. For an exponential metric (as in METR's worked example) it's the log growth rate. METR's definitions give total acceleration A = c/h ≈ 2, acceleration due to AI a = c/n, and implicitly non-AI acceleration b = n/h, with A = a·b. The "≈80%" is a claim about where n sits between h and c. The readings differ in which yardstick measures that.</span></i></p><p><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reading 1: share of the slope change (additive).</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (c − n)/(c − h) = 0.8, so n = h + 0.2(c − h). In METR's "years of progress" units this is concrete. At A = 2, Anthropic makes about one extra historical-year of progress per calendar year, and about 0.8 of that extra year is due to AI. Without AI it would make about 1.2 historical-years per year, which implies a ≈ 1.67. This is probably what's intended. METR's illustrative chart labels the two gaps "excess growth due to AI" and "excess growth due to non-AI factors," it annotates the slope ΔAECI/Δtime, the AECI trends are fit linearly, and one interviewee framed it the same way, attributing 50–80% of the slope change to Claude.</span></i></p><p><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reading 2: share of the log-acceleration (multiplicative).</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> ln a / ln A = 0.8, so a = A^0.8 ≈ 1.74 and b = A^0.2 ≈ 1.15. This is natural if you take METR's ratio definitions literally. Since a·b = A, the logs add exactly, so this split doesn't depend on which factor you remove first.</span></i></p><p><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reading 3: share of the excess multiplier.</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (a − 1)/(A − 1) = 0.8, so a = 1.8. It sounds natural ("AI provides 80% of the extra speedup"), but it isn't a decomposition. The non-AI share computed the same way is (b − 1)/(A − 1) ≈ 11%, so the two shares sum to 91%. An even more naive reading, a/A = 0.8, fails outright: if AI did nothing, a = 1 and you'd still get 1/A = 50%.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Implied AI acceleration a (and non-AI acceleration b), holding the 80% fixed:</span></i></p></blockquote><div class="iframe-widget"></div><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The readings agree near A ≈ 1, because ln x ≈ x − 1 there, and they diverge as A grows. At METR's central estimate the spread is modest. In METR's own illustrative numbers (h = ln 2, n = ln 3, c = ln 9), AI's share comes out to 73%, 60%, or 46% under readings 1, 2, and 3. The same picture could be described as "a majority" or "a minority" depending on the yardstick.</span></i></p><p><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why 1 versus 2 is really a modeling choice.</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> If AI and other factors add to the growth rate (c = h + Δ_AI + Δ_other), reading 1 is the clean split. If they multiply (c = h·a·b, for example because AI multiplies the productivity of whatever headcount, compute, and data you have), reading 1 becomes order-dependent. The counterfactual "remove AI, keep the non-AI factors" gives AI the entire interaction term h(a − 1)(b − 1). A Shapley split of the same numbers (A = 2, b = 1.2) gives AI about 73%, not 80%. Reading 2 has no such ambiguity under a multiplicative model.</span></i></p></blockquote><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: More analysis of higher-than-expected near-AC coding uplift</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SAR and TED-AI are also sensitive to the research taste slope (holding research taste at AC at the preset +0.5 SD), without changing the acceleration numbers much:</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To make it easier to understand the implications of this hypothesis, we compared its forecasts against some representative trajectories from the above methods, matching their Sep 25 taste and taste slope:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-46"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-46">[46]</a></sup></span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: How much would 1.7x acceleration in inputs to AI progress accelerate AI progress overall?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI software R&amp;D progress is the rate at which the AI company is improving the algorithms it uses to train models. However, training better models requires both algorithms and training compute. If we were making AI software R&amp;D progress 1.7x faster than normal, this would accelerate the algorithms but not the compute that goes into the final training runs, so the overall pace of progress would be less than 1.7x. We estimate that accelerating AI software R&amp;D by 1.7x would only accelerate overall progress by about 1.1x.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-47"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-47">[47]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Research labor uplift is even weaker than AI software R&amp;D uplift. Labor is one input to AI software R&amp;D, but compute to run experiments is another important input. If human labor was 1.7x faster than normal, this wouldn’t accelerate the experimental compute, so the pace of software progress would be less than 1.7x, and overall progress would be even less than that. We think that accelerating research labor by 1.7x would only accelerate overall progress by 1.03x.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-48"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-48">[48]</a></sup></span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: A minor fix to the AI Futures Model for better modeling when there’s acceleration in present day</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude writes:</span></p><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Takeaway: a fix to how the AI Futures Model converts the research taste slope moves the filtered results slightly earlier and barely changes anything else.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What changed: the taste slope is input as SDs/2025-year, but the model converted it using each trajectory’s pace of progress at present day (end of July 2026) rather than mid-2025. In trajectories that had already accelerated, like the filtered ones (1.2–1.4x the 2024–mid-2025 rate), this made the effective slope 20–30% lower than the parameter value. In typical trajectories the two rates match (median ratio 1.01). The conversion now uses the pace at mid-2025 (new parameter taste_slope_reference_date, default 2025.5).</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Effect: Daniel’s preset barely moves (SAR one week earlier). p(SAR by EO2027) goes from 72% to 77% (1.5x), 87% to 90% (1.7x), 88% to 92% (1.7x Nov 1, 1.2x May 1) and 95% to 96% (2x). The joint median SAR moves from Apr to Mar 2027; the other SAR medians are unchanged to the month, and TED-AI medians move up to a month earlier. The partial coding automation trajectory’s SAR moves from Jun to May 2027. The one-time-beta-decrease trajectory barely moves (still Mar 2027), because the conversion reads a mapping that excludes the beta shift (rate ratio 1.04).</span></i></p></blockquote><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: TasteVal-implied taste slope, AECI basis</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude writes:</span></p><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This appendix shows the fit behind the “TasteVal slope, AECI basis” row of the one-at-a-time table. TasteVal (arXiv 2610.06824) measures each model’s compute multiplier: the experiment compute the expert baseline needs to match the model’s final score, as a multiple of the model’s own compute budget. METR’s chart gives AECI only for Anthropic models, so each TasteVal model gets the AECI that METR’s fitted trend gives at its release date (+15 points per year before the October 7, 2025 break, +29 after; second plot). Release dates are read off TasteVal’s Figure 1.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We fit log(compute multiplier) against this AECI for the 17 frontier models TasteVal fits (GPT-4 to Opus 5.5), allowing one change in slope at the model where it best fits the data (largest Chow F-statistic, as TasteVal does). The change falls at GPT-5.2 (AECI ≈ 155, December 2025), the same model as in TasteVal’s own fits against release date and ECI. Before it, the multiplier doubled every 19.5 AECI points; since then, every 7.2 points (90% CI 5.4–9.0 from resampling the 11 post-break models). As a check, the same fit against release date reproduces TasteVal’s 3.0-month doubling time, which is about 7.2 points at 29 AECI points per year. A continuous kinked line gives 6.7 points per doubling. Using METR’s measured AECI for the six Anthropic models instead of the trend values gives 7.9, and fitting those six models alone, without a break, gives 5.6.</span></i></p></blockquote><img alt="Log-scale scatter plot titled 'TasteVal compute multiplier vs AECI, with a trend break at GPT-5.2'. Frontier models from GPT-4 (about 0.03× the human expert baseline) to Opus 5.5 (above 2×) are plotted against AECI from 110 to 180. The fitted trend is 19.5 AECI points per doubling before GPT-5.2 and 7.2 points per doubling since (90% CI 5.4–9.0)." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/jjwure2lbrz6kgwqxag8" /><img alt="Chart titled 'AECI assigned to each TasteVal model from METR's AECI trend', AECI against release date from 2023.5 to 2026.5. Models from GPT-4 to Opus 5.5 sit on METR's trend line, +15.4 per year before Oct 7, 2025 and +29.2 per year after. Hollow markers show the measured AECI of the same Anthropic models, each a few points below the line." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/fscqbbbnzaujsljbntzu" /><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: Software R&amp;D uplift extrapolation</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s another simple model: Extrapolating software R&amp;D uplift extrapolation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In </span><a href="https://x.com/RyanGreenblatt/status/2118584281215987798?a=6KOLXmdiGYzNpVJFEGw&amp;s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this tweet thread</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Ryan Greenblatt proposed a simple model for figuring out what software R&amp;D uplift is implied by an overall pace of progress estimate. Ryan used ~2x; we use the ~1.7x AI-attributed pace of progress from this post, since the method backs out how much AI is speeding up software R&amp;D.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we assume overall AI progress comes from ⅔ software and ⅓ hardware, then we get that speeding up the overall pace of progress by 1.7x requires speeding up software progress by 2.05x.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-49"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-49">[49]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then if we assume that software progress faces diminishing returns where each year of software progress is twice as hard, and that there has been an additional ~0.6 years of software progress, then you get that software R&amp;D uplift is (2.05 * 2^0.6 =) 3.1x in Nov 2026.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-50"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-50">[50]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we assume that as of May 2026 uplift was ~1.45x based on converting a ~1.2x AI-attributed pace of progress acceleration,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-51"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-51">[51]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and that SAR will correspond to a 25x uplift according to the AI Futures Model,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-52"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-52">[52]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">or ~45x according to the </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/labor-uplift-extrapolation"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SAR central estimate from the section above</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-53"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-53">[53]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">then we can do a naive exponential extrapolation:</span></p><img alt="Log-scale chart of excess AI software R&amp;D uplift (uplift minus 1) implied by the pace of progress: 1.45x on May 1, 2026 and 3.1x on Nov 1, 2026. An exponential through the two points, doubling about every 2.7 months, reaches the AI Futures Model's SAR level of 25x in Aug 2027 and a roughly 45x SAR estimate in Oct 2027." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/l4vrvjatedvnjjgmgww6" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This estimate is conservative in that it doesn’t account for automation speeding up the trend. However, it is extremely uncertain, given that we only have 2 data points which are both based on the METR report’s rough guesses. And the May data point is a very rough educated guess, rather than something explicitly stated by METR.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: How many years of AI progress in 2027 do the filtered AIFM runs predict?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We measure progress in 2027 as the ECI gained during 2027, in units of the average yearly ECI gain over 2024–25. Today's overall pace is ~2x (~1.7x AI-attributed), so if it held flat, 2027 would contain ~2 years of progress.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The AI Futures Model, filtered to runs with a ~1.2x AI-attributed pace on May 1 2026 and ~1.7x on Nov 1 2026, instead gives a median of ~5 years with Daniel's parameters and ~4 with Eli's:</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR described its range informally ("we would not be surprised if the true acceleration was closer to 1.3x or 3x the historical rate"), so we don't know what interval they meant.</span></p><img alt="Bar chart of years of AI progress per calendar year in AI Futures Model runs matching METR's estimates: 1.0 in 2025 and 1.4 in 2026 with both parameter sets, then 4.9 in 2027 with Daniel's parameters (50% CI 2.7 to 25) and 4.2 with Eli's (2.6 to 12)." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/tueqd7yjyhavdaabufvi" /><img alt="Cumulative distribution, on a log scale, of years of 2024–25-pace progress made during 2027 across AI Futures Model runs matching METR's estimates. The median is 4.9 years with Daniel's parameters (78 runs) and 4.2 with Eli's (76 runs); today's roughly 2x overall pace held flat would give 2." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/x97c8fxmwfj7fv7nxjkq" /><img alt="Pace of progress over time, on a log scale, in AI Futures Model runs with about 1.2x pace on May 1 and 1.7x on Nov 1, 2026. The median pace climbs through 2027 to roughly 4 to 5x around mid-2027, then eases to about 2.5x by mid-2028, with wide 10th to 90th percentile bands. Daniel's and Eli's median curves nearly coincide." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/yem0vmzaybtk5lr3xlia" /><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-1"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-1">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By Superhuman AI Researcher (SAR) we mean: “an AI that matches the research taste of the best human researchers at an AGI company and can fully automate its coding.”</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-2"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-2">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We define it more precisely </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/operationalization"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-3"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-3">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also think we are likely to see something like </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/appendix-how-many-years-of-ai-progress-in-2027-do-the-filtered-aifm-runs-predict"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">5 years’ worth of historical AI progress in 2027</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (50% CI: 2.7 to 25 years). This is based on filtering the AI Futures Model with Daniel’s parameters to condition on 1.7x pace of progress in Nov 2026 and 1.2x in May.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-4"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-4">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See e.g., </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/daniels-forecast"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Daniel’s forecast writeup below</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for more factors.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also recommend </span><a href="https://www.planned-obsolescence.org/p/ai-is-dramatically-accelerating-ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this piece</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> which also analyzes the implications of the METR report.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-6"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-6">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It wasn’t just our predictions about the future that were too low; we also now think our estimates of the pace of progress in August 2026 were too low. Also note that this was the prediction implied by the model and parameters assigned by specific people, which may well have been lower than their all-things-considered view if they had been specifically asked about this question.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-7"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-7">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While the simple research extrapolation has a median Sep 2026 research taste of 1.5 SDs below the median AGI company researcher.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The ECI trend in the trajectory doesn’t match the AECI historical trajectory well, because the AIFM can’t model a slope increase that was initially mostly from non-AI-attributable factors.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR writes: “</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We guess that a majority (perhaps ≈80%) of the current (October 2026) acceleration is due to AI.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This judgment is given with substantial uncertainty, but is based on a variety of lines of evidence on contributions from AI and other causes. We believe AI’s contribution has grown relatively more important over time, and do not think the other factors we investigated would explain the sustained acceleration. The evidence of speedup from the usage of AI we describe below also seems compatible with a large contribution.” 1.7 is roughly 80% of the way to 2 from the baseline rate of progress in log space. More on this in </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/i/224227629/appendix-ambiguity-in-80-of-current-acceleration-is-due-to-ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">an appendix</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-10"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-10">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR says: “We are confident that the acceleration was not due to an acceleration in the scaling of training compute.” Based on what we can determine externally from financial reporting, it seems like R&amp;D compute grew around 3x 2024 → 2025 and 4.2x 2025 → 2026 (accounting for changes in allocation).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-11"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-11">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The baseline is roughly 14 points per year, and 14*1.7=24/year is achieved roughly in April.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-12"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-12">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that the AI does not need to dominate humans across every subskill of research taste. It is enough for the AI to have research taste such that the AI company would overall prefer to use only AIs for research taste than to use only copies of top humans for research taste (more precisely, the copies would have aggregate research taste as good as the top human, while retaining the same distribution of specializations as current human researchers).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-13"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-13">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Or actually, R&amp;D parity only requires research taste slightly below average because coding skills will be ahead.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-14"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-14">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, the acceleration being AI-attributable is evidence for more aggressive research taste level and slope parameters.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-15"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-15">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude estimates based on the charts in the report that it was internally deployed on roughly Sep 25.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-16"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-16">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My best guess is that AIs aren’t yet contributing enough via taste that we have that much evidence about the taste slope via an update toward more gradual acceleration. However: (a) it’s plausible to me that AIs are starting to contribute to taste in some sense, via mechanisms like (1) better integrating information across teams leading to more fruitful experiment ideas or (2) brainstorming lots of ideas quickly and (b) even if the taste is currently quite low, I can hackily represent the general update toward slower takeoff based on gradual acceleration via lowering the taste slope here.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-17"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-17">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Time to SAR is the remaining taste gap divided by the slope, so draws where Model 4 is already near or past SAR arrive early almost regardless of the slope, while draws with a large gap are pulled earlier by a steep slope about as often as they're pushed later by a shallow one.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-18"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-18">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the top researcher is much more valuable than the median researcher. The difference between the 99.9th percentile and average of a lognormal distribution with median 1 and 99.9th percentile 1,000 is ~80x (the average is ~12x the median).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-19"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-19">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">k is directly proportional to r, i.e. the number of doublings of software efficiency you get from a doubling of cumulative research. In the terms of </span><a href="https://elasticity.institute/rsi-paper.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Economics of RSI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, k = ln 2 × r × ε_C,A. (With ε_C,A ≈ 3.9 AECI points per e-fold in our calibration; the paper's 6.5 is for Epoch's ECI).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-20"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-20">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also called ε_R,C, and "research effort's elasticity to AI capabilities”.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-21"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-21">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I.e., I’ll assume that d is itself a linear function of ECI going forward.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-22"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-22">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These numbers imply that r (doublings of software efficiency per doublings of research) is roughly 3.3, and that k is roughly 9 AECI points per doubling of research.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-23"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-aFCFuAEgBt6g5hZwp-23">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From the labor uplift section above: raising all researchers to the research taste of the best researcher is worth ~100x (the top researcher vs. the average researcher), or ~500x after accounting for complementarity between AIs and humans; To convert labor uplift into software R&amp;D uplift, we use Ryan Greenblatt’s production function for software progress, labor^0.55 * compute^0.45, with experiment compute held fixed: 1,000^0.55 = ~45x. ### [Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang Had an Affair With a Chinese Spy](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CjmuZz8QgGyqxkCxr/nvidia-ceo-jensen-huang-had-an-affair-with-a-chinese-spy)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Well, sure, it sounds bad when you put it like that.</p>
<p>It is indeed quite bad. National security has been compromised. He must resign.</p>
<p>Nvidia underperformed the Nasdaq roughly 7% on the news. <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/opinion/newsletters/2026-11-09/is-the-nvidia-affair-securities-fraud">Matt Levine has his usual ‘but is this securities fraud?’</a> style coverage of such events.</p>
