### [Live disagreements with people around me](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dQJ3ArHMLxqu8rptt/live-disagreements-with-people-around-me)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>I’m pretty interested in conveying the costs of letting AI development proceed unchecked to the general public. In relation to this, I feel like I run into a lot of disagreements with people around me.</p><p>Misc very quickly written points on which my own impression has seemed very different from others in recent months:</p><p><strong>1. It’s way, way, way more important that we stop dangerous AI soon than that we ensure that we don’t stop AI too much</strong></p><p>I have the sense the sophisticated and worldly position here is to be very concerned about ‘over-regulation’ and about not doing exactly the right regulations. But it’s an obvious pragmatic fact that the stakes of overregulation are minuscule compared to the stakes of underregulating here. And the stakes of mis-regulation are very often going to be unintended slowing, which is in the same bucket.</p><p><strong>2. If we stopped AI development, the risk of not starting up again for an extremely long time is low</strong></p><p>This one just feels kind of crazy to me, like I may be missing something. In combination with the above point, it feels like if I met someone who was on fire, and they didn’t want me to put out the fire, in case they couldn’t figure out how to get warm again, ever. Like, forever is a long time, and if this thing is so great, you’re probably not going to forget about it! It’s like you think the current instantiation of yourself (or society) is so much more agentic than your future selves, that it’s worth huge costs to keep power in the current version’s hands.</p><p><strong>3. Getting the public involved is not very dangerous</strong></p><p>Some people seem to think of the public as like a kind of wild and crazy god one could awaken, with very high variance results. I think this doesn’t give them nearly enough credit. While I disagree with the public on quite a lot of points, I think disagreements are a lot more salient than agreements. We might disagree a lot on exactly how the FDA proceeds or who gets in how much trouble for doing what with which drugs. But we agree that broadly medical interventions and drugs are dangerous for people, and on wanting more safety around things that kill people, and I bet a lot of the details I don’t notice are better than nothing. And in a specific case where the default is insanely dangerous, bringing more people with power into the room, who are liable to stop things that are dangerous, and couldn’t really at this point make it much worse, seems pretty safe.</p><p><strong>4. Public comms is not that rife with downside risks</strong></p><p>There is a general sense that it is riskier to donate to groups hoping to talk to the public, because they can have large scale effects and make everyone look bad. So people donate their money to ‘safe bets’ such as e.g. technical safety. I think this is generally very miscalibrated. Talking to the public incompetently isn’t as far as I can tell much more likely to have reputational risks than doing other stuff incompetently, because everyone is in public. I don’t see many examples of this happening at a scale larger than the positive effects.</p><p><strong>5. Pausing advanced AI soon greatly increases our chances of surviving</strong></p><p>For one thing, I’d say the world’s degree of not giving a shit about safety in this case of advanced AI right now is extremely unique among issues, and if we wait for long enough for people to reckon with what’s happening at all, it is very unlikely to proceed in such wildly reckless way later on.</p><p>Also, I just expect us to make a lot of progress in understanding current AI models and organizing ourselves to not do crazy things if we have another decade to work out the kinks.</p><p><strong>6. We don’t need a plan before we pause</strong></p><p>People seem to think we do. I don’t know what’s up with that.</p><p><strong>7. Raising the alarm is very important at the moment</strong></p><p>I feel like people around me mostly agree that raising the alarm is good, but I’m surprised by the frequency of ambivalence about it. Partly this is to do with earlier points and related things, such as ‘what if people become too scared about AI and something bad happens’.</p><p><strong>8. Working on marginally improving safety of models at the AI companies is not remotely competitive with raising the alarm at the moment</strong></p><p>From my current vantage, it seems like people building AI do not mostly expect if AI left our control soon for it to be solidly aligned with a high probability. And they don’t seem to think we are close to that. So for a high chance of success, we really need to avoid that happening and we can make a lot of progress on that on the margin. Whereas if we know we are far from safety right now, adding marginal safety in a scenario where we are about to lose control seems pretty hopeless.</p><p><strong>9. Doing advocacy doesn’t seem to have adverse effects on people doing it</strong></p><p>I’ve heard a couple of times that people who do advocacy seem to go crazy. I don’t know of anything that seems like solid evidence of this, and weak or circumstantial evidence seems to only involve a handful of people.</p><p><em>And one where I feel like I haven’t heard so much disagreement lately:</em></p><p><strong>10. It matters what the public think / political will is growable</strong></p><p>I’ve been interested in talking to the public for a long time, and people have often disagreed with me. My sense is that they have often thought it seemed pointless. Because the public doesn’t have power. But I think the public does have power, and not just because they can ultimately vote. People care what other people think, including elites, and experts and people with power. Nobody is coming to their views in a vacuum. This matters.</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dQJ3ArHMLxqu8rptt/live-disagreements-with-people-around-me#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Eyesight as a replicator, with genes as its vehicle](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FLAWND7tXutaPw9BQ/eyesight-as-a-replicator-with-genes-as-its-vehicle)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Or: The payload lens on Darwin</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tl;dr: What is the most natural teleological shorthand to use, when thinking about evolution?</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The “gene-centered view” of e.g. Dawkins says: genes are “trying” to increase the number of exact copies of themselves. And organisms are “vehicles” genes use to survive.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The “payload lens” (which I’ll be pitching) says: adaptive payloads (such as eyesight or fair meiosis) are “trying” to become deeper, stabler attractor basins that can team up with other adaptive payloads. And genes are “vehicles” these payloads use to survive.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Epistemic status: I haven’t done much of a lit search, so I’m claiming neither that any of this is original nor that it is vetted/established; I’m laying it out partly because I want to be able to build on it in some later essays about what we humans are (and what some possible AIs might be) other than optimizers. (However, I don’t mostly get to that here; and I hope this stuff may also be interesting in its own right.)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefj381gjbqzg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnj381gjbqzg">[1]</a></sup></span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Angles on Darwin</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Darwinian evolution (as operationalized by Lewontin in his 1970 paper </span><a href="https://www.zoology.ubc.ca/let/pdfs/Lewontin1970.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Units of Selection</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which helped organize the field) is the theory that intricate adaptations (such as those in Earth’s living organisms) tend to arise when a process satisfies three principles:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Variation (organisms vary)</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Differential reproduction (different variations have different reproductive odds)</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Heredity (new organisms resemble their parents more than they resemble random population members, including in reproductive success).</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lewontin stresses that these principles can apply at any of several levels, e.g. genes, cells, gametes, organisms, or populations. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My business in this essay will be to contrast two such concretizations: “genes as replicators,” which is the standard lens associated with </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adaptation_and_Natural_Selection"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Williams</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Selfish_Gene"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dawkins</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and “adaptive payloads as replicators”, which I have not </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/vNBxmcHpnozjrJnJP/no-one-knows-what-science-doesn-t-know"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">personally</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> seen in the literature. I will ask how much sense each lens lets us make of the living world.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Genes as replicators</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">DNA strands can be copied with high fidelity, so that a given chunk of functional DNA (a “gene”) is often exactly preserved from parent to child. Such genes are a natural and common way to concretize 1-3. Under this lens, 1-3 becomes:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Variation: DNA sequences vary</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Differential reproduction: DNA sequences reproduce when their organisms reproduce, and some sequence-variants help their organisms reproduce more than others do</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Heredity: a copied sequence matches its parent sequence, apart from rare mutations.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Following others, I will call this lens the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene-centered_view_of_evolution"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">gene-centered view</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I believe this lens captures much, but far from all, of the ways 1-3 can help account for life’s variety.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Adaptive payloads as replicators</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Adaptive payloads are innovations that make their organisms better at reproducing, such as eyesight, fair meiosis, or a preference for eating sugars. Adaptive payloads also constitute what Daniel Dennett calls “design work.”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0ov09hygyji"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0ov09hygyji">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can get an alternate concretization of Darwin’s 1-3 by taking adaptive payloads as our replicators. Under this lens, 1-3 becomes:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Variation: Adaptations vary across organisms (e.g, different organisms have more or less functional eyesight)</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Differential reproduction: adaptive payloads reproduce (noisily) when their organisms reproduce; their organisms' reproduction depends partly on the payloads’ presence</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Heredity: the adaptations in the offspring bear a noisy resemblance to the adaptations in the parent.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Adaptive payloads are not copied exactly, as genes are; but they nonetheless possess </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">enough</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> parent-child resemblance to satisfy “heredity” in Lewontin’s sense. (Being a “replicator” in this sense does not require exact copying.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I will call this the “payload lens.” </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is well-established that the gene-centered view is useful; I will spend this essay arguing that the payload lens carries some additional value (though the reverse is also true; the genes lens is irreplaceable).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxguuy06jol"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxguuy06jol">[3]</a></sup></span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Example: eyesight </span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consider eyesight. More exactly, consider the progression over time that eyesight has in a particular lineage – a progression across many millions of years from the first eyespots to primitive eyes to more effective, robust eyes that work in various particular contexts.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The payload lens makes it easier to notice some facts about eyesight’s trajectory.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eyesight is a stable attractor</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let’s jump in in the middle of the story, by attending to eyesight in a particular population of raccoons (say).</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Imagine some process messes intermittently with some raccoons’ eyesight – perhaps there’s a new attractive food source that contains a vision-impairing toxin. Or perhaps this population recently began mating with a new population of quasi-raccoons that had previously been separated by a mountain range and the new gene-combinations sometimes fail to make fully functional eyes.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this context, evolution won’t have to look through as many new mutations before it finds one that can counter the trouble (since this is a simpler change; fixing a previously-working machine is easier than building a machine from scratch). Also, existing variants that help with the new circumstance may quickly approach fixation (since a previously-working machine that is slightly broken offers strong selection gradients toward fixing the machine).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thus, the presence of useful eyes will cause selection toward more robust eyes. Importantly, this rapid selection toward robust eyes happens mostly </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">because eyesight is already present;</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> animals with no eyes might evolve eyesight, but the selection would be far slower.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eyesight is thus a stable attractor (and this is easy to describe in terms of “eyesight,” i.e. when focusing on eyesight as an adaptive payload, and hard to describe in terms of genes directly, since e.g. “restores eyesight” could happen via any of thousands of genes).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Of course, it is stable only so long as it is useful; when e.g. naked mole-rats move into a niche without light, entropy gradually disrupts the payload. The “eyesight attractor” requires constant selection to preserve its adaptive payload.)</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The eyesight of Theseus</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The “ship of Theseus” is a ship from a famous thought experiment; the ship stayed arguably “itself” across a huge period of time, even as every piece of it eventually wore out and was replaced.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eyesight is a kind of “ship of Theseus”; its “stable attractor” nature can be preserved across huge periods of evolutionary time, and despite massive changes to the genes that initially supported it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Or, well, that’s a theory-driven approximation. To attempt empirical accuracy:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The opsin family (a family of photosensitive proteins) has been conserved since before eyes’ beginnings. This does not much fit the payload lens. Early on, evolution found a good method for turning light into a rapid signal within a cell – a method that did not lead easily into an attractor of much better methods – and it has kept that method.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefc6jbyo0uak"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnc6jbyo0uak">[4]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Pax6 gene (a “master control gene” that activates many other genes that together build eyes) is also highly conserved: if a mouse version of Pax6 is expressed in a fruit fly’s leg, the fruit fly forms an additional </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fruit fly</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> eye on its leg. That is: the small thousands of genes involved in eye-formation in the mouse, and the small thousands of genes involved in making quite different fruit-fly eyes in the fruit fly, are still triggered by genes similar enough that copy-pasting the mouse one works on the fruit fly. I believe this </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">does</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> fit the payload hypothesis, as ~any change to the function of the Pax6 gene would cut off huge chunks of the “eyesight” payload at once.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most other things </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">do</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> get substituted out over time. (E.g. our distant ancestors had four cone opsin classes, for color vision; our ancestors lost two of these while nocturnal, but then Old World primates got a new one by duplicating-and-mutating one of their existing ones. Or, for another example: many birds swapped out most of the ancestral crystallins used to make their transparent eye-lenses for a new one called δ-crystallin while retaining the same eye-shape and design; this new one was a recruited copy of a metabolic gene.)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2xx2y978a3n"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2xx2y978a3n">[5]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, eyesight is a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">partial</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> “ship of Theseus”, where most planks can be substituted out but Pax6 can’t (lest the whole ship lose its function) and opsins can’t (because they’re already quite optimized, and there doesn’t seem to be a hill-climbable path from there to a different and better solution).</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Example: meiotic fairness as replicator</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For an example of a payload that is even less tied to particular genes, consider meiotic fairness. As you may recall, meiosis is the process by which eukaryotic organisms create haploid gametes (sperm or eggs), and it is carefully “fair” in the sense that (almost) all alleles have almost exactly a 50% chance of making it into any given gamete. What keeps meiosis fair?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Well: what keeps it fair is the outcome of an ongoing “race” between cheating and cheat-prevention.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On the one hand, genes are constantly under selective pressure to find a way into a larger share of gametes. And so new “cheater” genes are continually arising. (Biologists call this cheating “meiotic drive.”)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On the other hand, all the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">other</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> genes are under selective pressure to prevent “cheater” genes from being able to succeed. Why? Because “cheater” genes typically have, as their mechanism for cheating, some pattern that is quite bad for the organism’s normal gamete-formation process and so for its fertility – and thus if a gene can prevent other genes from cheating, it can prevent its organism from having serious reproductive trouble. (Some common cheater mechanisms: poison-antidote, where if an allele isn’t copied into the gamete the gamete often dies; centromeric drive, where an allele aggressively pulls itself into the forming egg and thereby causes segregation errors; overreplication, where a gene copies itself within the somatic cells also in ways that can cause cancer.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The observed outcome of this process is that cheater genes are quite rare. And importantly for our purposes:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This equilibrium is maintained via the continual selection of </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">new</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> anti-cheater genes (to counter newly evolved cheats); the likely persistence of this equilibrium thus can’t be accounted for by selection pressure toward maintaining current genes; and</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The selective pressures that maintain this equilibrium are </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">caused in significant part by the equilibrium itself</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> – it is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">because</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> meiosis is typically fair that “cheater” genes are so greatly outnumbered by fair genes (genes that would also suffer the cost of the cheaters’ fertility damage, and don’t get to share in the cheat’s upside), such that the forces of fairness prevail.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, meiotic fairness is another place where the payload lens pays some rent that is not already being paid by the gene-centered view. (We have a stable attractor, and a “ship of Theseus” robustness to the swapping-out of alleles.)</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">much</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of biological evolution makes more sense through the payload lens? </span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Are eyes and meiotic fairness unusual in this way, or is most biological adaptation similar? That is: do the adaptive payloads behind most biological function tend to have “their own momentum” to a greater extent than one might expect if one looked only through the gene-centered lens? By “momentum,” I mean the tendency of a payload to persist and continue to accrue more design work, more function, more robustness-to-disruption. If many adaptive payloads have their own momentum in this way – why? And does the payload lens also shed light on other aspects of biological evolution, beyond the “many payloads are stable attractors” point?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t fully know; but I’d like to lay out some considerations.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Momentum” examples probably include most cases where multiple genes combine to make a “simple” or “form-like” effect</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Payloads probably have their own momentum (ship of Theseus style, i.e. beyond the momentum possessed by the genes that make them up) in most cases where: a) the phenotype ends up somehow “simple” or “form-like” in some approximate respect; and b) this order isn’t simply inherited from physics or chemistry; it required multi-pronged evolutionary design work to create this simplicity.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some examples of “simple”: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Homeostatic settings: e.g. humans maintain an internal body temperature of roughly 37°C (across many different internal organs, and across time), and humans maintain blood potassium levels in a fairly tight range across time. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Simple functions: cardiovascular systems “deliver oxygen and remove CO2”, tendons “attach muscles to bones to enable movement,” a ball-in-socket joint is an approximate “sphere,” etc. – in each of these cases there’s a simply describable approximate function that applies across a range of molecules, mechanisms, etc.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eyesight and fair meiosis, as above.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why I expect momentum where I see designed simplicity:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If this design work happened, there is (or was) an advantage in it. This advantage may well continue. (E.g., eyesight looks obviously designed, and this happens because it’s useful; apart from e.g. naked mole-rats and cave fish it mostly stays useful, and so there’ll be continued selection to make eyesight work well and robustly.)</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Once a payload A is stably around, other payloads B may come to depend on it; such dependence will increase the selective pressure making A robust (since disruptions to A will now also impair B)</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If a design is simple, it’ll be easier for new mechanisms to build on it (either in the sense of depending on it, as in #2 here, or in the sense of aiding it and so strengthening whatever fitness advantage it is conferring, as in #1 here.)</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eHeHAyNQWmg4kHgEL/all-interesting-behavior-is-overdetermined"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Related</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">)</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pathways between adaptations</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Darwin’s core achievement is to shed light on where all the design work in life comes from. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So far, we haven’t touched most of this. Even if eyes, fair meiosis, tendons, warm-blooded temperature regulation, etc. tend to gather momentum once started … how did they get started? Does finding some of these attractors make it easier to find others? Does the payload lens help explain life’s ability to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">locate</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the functional structures we see in today’s lifeforms, or does it only explain life’s ability to keep hold of payloads already found?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I suspect the payload lens helps with the finding, as well as the keeping. I will next argue for this.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Payload geometry: islands or ridge-lines?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The question, broadly, is what the actual landscape of evolutionary biology looks like through the payload lens.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794302206/lexical_client_uploads/zzo1r219ga1dw4uwdn3x.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is it more like a set of isolated islands?</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794302206/lexical_client_uploads/kpdws8xp7etmijgib2y2.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Or are the islands connected by traversable ridges, where some payloads make other payloads either easier to start building (e.g. eyes may make it easier to build a predator-triggered startle reflex) or more worthwhile to keep building (e.g. eyes make brains more useful)?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(The gene-centered view has its own answer here: the more genes are around, the easier it is for new adaptations to arise by duplicating an existing gene and then allowing the new copy to specialize in a new direction. Does the payload lens have anything to add?)</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Payloads change which other payloads are worth it</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Adaptive payloads can make other adaptive payloads more useful. For example, eyes can make brains more useful no matter what genes implement the eyes.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefr9kpgpb8h4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnr9kpgpb8h4">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (Likewise, brains can make eyes more useful; there’s no point in gathering data you can’t interpret.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also, it is easier for evolution to locate structures with a bigger payoff – e.g. if the reward for better brains is high, it’ll be easier to find a path toward them, since it’ll be acceptable to do so in ways that cost more calories or temporarily disrupt other structures.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Toeholds without genes: The Baldwin effect</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Often, the hardest part about evolving a given adaptive payload is the “getting started” part: once you </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">have</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> an adaptation (even a weak or intermittent one) there’s often a nice climbable selective gradient that can get you to better versions, but that same gradient can’t well-lead you to the thing’s start because there’s not much advantage in having the barest beginnings of some adaptations.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My claim in this section is this: the “having a toehold makes it way easier to evolve the rest of an adaptive payload” phenomenon can hold </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">even when the initial toehold is triggered by the environment, rather than by genes!</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To see this, consider a population of crows who live near walnut trees. The crows would like to eat the walnuts; and it is theoretically possible for the crows to evolve genes that predispose them to take the complex actions required to crack the walnuts (namely, to drop walnuts onto hard surfaces from very high in the air).</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794315093/lexical_client_uploads/w0e02qrzwskzoalaniem.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Absent learning, evolution would need a very long time to locate genes for such a complex behavior because its reward function is sharply all-or-nothing. (Doing </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">part</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of the walnut-cracking ritual gets you zero walnuts.)</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794315133/lexical_client_uploads/uj8gpf3kp0bqmnurvouv.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fortunately for the crows, they </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">can</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> learn. And so they occasionally learn to crack the walnuts via imitation and reasoning, even without genetic help. And genetic variations that </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">predispose them slightly</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> toward the right walnut-cracking rituals let them succeed more often, making a nice climbable gradient toward genes that encode more and more of what they need to do. This is called the “Baldwin effect.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The above example is theoretical, but Geoffrey Hinton and Steven Nowlan have a nice </span><a href="https://www.cs.toronto.edu/~hinton/absps/evolution.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with simulated examples, and biologists have also demonstrated some </span><a href="https://www.chd.ucsd.edu/_files/winter2009/waddington-assimilation.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">real</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.eurekalert.org/news-releases/461080"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">examples</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Biologists disagree with each other about how common and significant Baldwin effect examples are, though my personal guess is “common.”</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cascades of toeholds</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In math, it is frequently claimed that a student needs “mathematical maturity” before they can properly study upper division mathematics. That is: they need, not any particular knowledge, but an elusive something that comes from working through a certain amount of other math. Similarly, the fifth type of partner dance you learn will typically be much easier than the first, even when you aren’t re-using any particular dance-step patterns in a way you can point to.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More broadly, some skills make it easier to learn other skills via resembling them in some subtle fashion, even where there’s no obvious “this is a step in that” relationship.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I suspect that many payloads, in combination with many environments and in-environment learning, can similarly make it easier to get a toehold toward various new payloads. (“Learning,” here, does not have to be in the manner of an animal with a nervous system; it can also be e.g. an organism of whatever sort adapting to excess salinity using its homeostatic mechanisms, or any other within-lifetime adaptation.) Skills that are from some angles separate are from some other angles reminiscent of one another, such that there may be a graph whereby learning some payloads gives a lineage a toehold toward others, which then acquire their own momentum and give the lineage toeholds toward still others. (Examples: the chemical receptors → light-detectors example we met earlier; feathers being used initially for warmth but offering a toehold toward flight. Biologists call this “exaptation.”)</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why I care</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are several reasons I care about the payload lens, some of which I hope to reach in later posts. But I’ll touch lightly on some now.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A toy model of something corrigibility-adjacent</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A payload </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> will often “want” another payload </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">B</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to be thriving, to be accurately oriented to its surroundings, and to do what </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">B</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> thinks best rather than being controlled by </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (in cases where </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">B</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> has important priors or skills that </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">doesn’t, which will be many cases). In less teleological language: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> will often survive-and-propagate better if </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">B</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> can do this (and if </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> can avoid interfering with </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">B’s</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> doing this).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thus, the payload lens offers (I think) a toy model of something corrigibility-adjacent. I’m interested in this model because I might want people to grow artificial systems that respect other people or payloads in this way, and because I would personally like to learn to relate to other people and payloads in this way. Also I’m enough of a LessWronger to want mathy models of my deep desires where I can get them, and “team up well while respecting what others are, and while having them respect me” is among my deep desires. (Though, to be clear, this is at best a hint as to how to model it. Or a “toehold,” optimistically.)</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A hint at where simple structures and functions may come from</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I also sketched a bit of where “simple” or “intelligible” structures within organisms might appear from. I like having toy models of this. I’m fairly sure there’s more here.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What am I? What am I for?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Am I an optimizer with a fixed utility function, wishing, if I can, to control the lightcone and transform it according to my own preexisting values? Or something else?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Personally, I like to think of myself as a payload, with my heart looking after many of the “useful, payload-y” parts of genes and memes, and with this payload having reached me via the efforts of many genetic and memetic ancestors who worked hard and choosily to figure out what parts to pass on, and what new payloads to join with as they did so.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And I’d like to think of me, this moment, my choices, as a case where many of these payloads are somehow collaborating with each other in choosing paths forward that can honor most of what I carry, and in looking for what other payloads (even of alien origin) I might wish, and be able to, team up freely with.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a choice on my part – I’m taking poetic inspiration from the biology I’ve sketched above; it’s not forced.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnj381gjbqzg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefj381gjbqzg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I do owe substantial intellectual debts to: </span><a href="https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.0701035104?url_ver=Z39.88-2003&amp;rfr_id=ori%3Arid%3Acrossref.org&amp;rfr_dat=cr_pub++0pubmed"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gerhart and Kirschner</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (whom I last read in 2007, but whose work stayed with me); </span><a href="https://drmichaellevin.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Michael Levin</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; Daniel Dennett; and Friedrich Hayek, although in each case I got patterns of thought more than citeable specifics. I would also like to thank a Claude Fable 5.1 instance who helped me with biology facts and pointers, drew my diagrams, and helped me tighten the concepts. Remaining errors are of course my own.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0ov09hygyji"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0ov09hygyji">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Design work" here means "stuff (such as a watch, or an intricate bit of biological function) that must be the result of some sort of "research and design"-type process, such as a human engineer, biological evolution, or memetic trial and error across a large population.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dennett's concept originates (in some sense) in William Paley's famous thought experiment in which a man, walking across a heath, happens upon a watch and infers, as he looks at the intricate ticking gears (even without having seen a watch before), that there must somewhere be a watch-maker. Dawkins later riffed off this thought experiment by referring to evolution as a "blind watch-maker," as did Dennett with his notion of "design work," which he uses to describe anything that visibly contains R&amp;D.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnxguuy06jol"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefxguuy06jol">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To see at once that the genes lens is great, notice e.g. that we today see no life without nucleic acid sequences – evolution has not produced lineages that can thrive and produce offspring without genes.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnc6jbyo0uak"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefc6jbyo0uak">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Although, evolution didn’t get opsins from nowhere; opsins evolved from previous sensor-proteins that cells used to detect chemicals, and the thing where a previous payload (G-protein-coupled receptors) made it easier for this new payload (vision) to arise… </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">does</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> remind me of the payload lens. See the section </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/editPost?postId=FLAWND7tXutaPw9BQ&amp;key=bf6ebeed0bbc8fccf2705769d2054f#Cascades_of_toeholds" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cascades of toeholds</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2xx2y978a3n"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2xx2y978a3n">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apparently biologists have a term for a related sort of substitution –  “developmental system drift,” as introduced by </span><a href="https://science.umd.edu/biology/haaglab/True&amp;Haag_DSD.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">True and Haag, 2001</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; the authors’ claim, like mine, is that sometimes selection pressures cause aspects of a phenotype to remain stable despite substantial changes in genes. (Unlike me, the authors know lots of biology and can root their generalizations in empirics.)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnr9kpgpb8h4"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefr9kpgpb8h4">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Except insofar as they change the eyes-brains interface</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FLAWND7tXutaPw9BQ/eyesight-as-a-replicator-with-genes-as-its-vehicle#comments">Discuss</a>

## Engineering

### [Fragments: November 10](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-11-10.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>It’s that time of the year when the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/radar">November Thoughtworks Technology Radar</a> appears. Again my colleagues have taken a sweep of the technologies that our teams have used in the field, and pulled together a summary of what’s popped up in the last six months. It’s been a busy half-year, one that again has been dominated by AI, and that’s reflected in the many AI blips on the radar.</p>

<p>The mass of blips makes it hard to develop a coherent story around trends, but Chris Ford has done a fine job of the writing the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/insights/blog/technology-strategy/macro-trends-tech-industry-november-2026">accompanying article on macro trends</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>One team of passionate people experimenting with agents can make almost anything work. They share a context, a credit card and a mental model. They review each other’s work because they sit together, and they know what to check and how much latitude to give the coding agent.</p>

  <p>Two hundred people, though, cannot work that way. The moment agentic AI becomes an operating model rather than a pilot, a second layer of problems appears: How is context shared between teams? What’s the token bill? How do we deal with increased review demands? What’s the agent allowed to reach, read and change? And who’s now responsible for the harness everyone depends on?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Experience so far suggests that the questions that enterprises face are about: shared context, token cost, cognitive load, security, and platform building. While these issues are distinct, they are not separate, so strategies to answer them must take all of them into account. I also echo Chris in reminding us that however impressive these tools are, we must remember that while we have to rethink systems and workflows to utilize these technologies, they should be built to serve humans - and we should all benefit from its bounty.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Ben Thompson’s Stratechery newsletter <a href="https://stratechery.com/">reflects on  the recent election’s impact on data centers</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>I watch a lot of sports, and particularly this fall, watched a lot of baseball. My Milwaukee Brewers — excuse me, my World Champion Milwaukee Brewers — demanded my full attention, which also meant I saw a lot of campaign ads, and by far the most common topic, at least here in Wisconsin, was data centers. Moreover, it was never entirely clear which way it cut politically: both parties were anti-data center, accusing their opponent of being the opposite.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He points out that this opposition is recent, and tied to a shift in focus from the internet and streaming movies to AI:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>you know, the technology that in the best case scenario is going to take everyone’s jobs, and in the worst case scenario end the human race.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>There’s going to be a lot said about how Democratic officials will be at the heart of blocking data center building, but as his point above about campaign ads illustrates, the opposition to data centers is (at least at the moment) a bi-partisan opposition.</p>

<p>He follows up with “ten takes” from these consequences. The big beneficiaries of this opposition to building data centers will be those who own the data centers that have already been built. The opposition will reduce supply of tokens, meaning that token costs will go up, thus increasing the profits for those who already have their token generators plugged in and humming.</p>

<p>There’s also benefits to those who aren’t constrained by the need to build more big data centers. Other countries aren’t affected by a change in US policy, China is the most talked-about case here, but it’s not the only country that may benefit from a drop in US data center building. Apple may also benefit, if it means more AI work is pushed to edge devices that are their territory, in addition to having not sunk so many dollars into AI-specific investments.</p>

<p>To his analysis, I’ll add that this may also have a somewhat counter-intuitive effect on regulation, and corporate attitudes to it. The common expectation is that businesses are opposed to government regulation, since that interferes with their free pursuit of profit. That’s true to an extent, but it’s quite common to see established businesses lobby (quietly) in favor of regulation that acts as a barrier to new competitors. So I wouldn’t be at all surprised to see companies that have already built data centers to be pushing for regulations that impede or prevent new ones from being built.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I’ve been a long-time fan of Hadley Wickham, for how he has tamed the rather messy world of R programming into a <a href="https://tidyverse.org/">well-designed tidyverse</a>. He is another thoughtful voice to listen to as we move into the world of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticEngineering.html">agentic engineering</a>, in this case by trying to figure out <a href="https://substack.hadley.nz/p/how-much-do-tokens-really-cost">what the real cost of tokens is</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>I hear a lot of suspicion that AI tokens are unsustainably priced and that frontier labs are heavily subsidising costs with their VC money. They want to get us hooked now, so they can pull the rug out from under us whenever they want to make a bunch of money. Is that true? My sense is that it’s mostly not. But can I back that up with some data?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>His conclusion is that it doesn’t seem that token costs are significantly subsidized on per-token API basis, but they are with subscription plans. (He caveats his analysis, saying it doesn’t take into account recouping the costs of training the models.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>There’s a creepy excitement at the thought that models are clever enough to edit the weights of models, an activity that <a href="https://rikiyat.github.io/blog/weight-space-programming/">Takehi et al refer to as Weight-Space Programming</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Just by looking at the raw weights, GPT-6 Astra can tell what a network was trained to do. And without any environment feedback, it has no trouble writing, from scratch, small game-playing networks that outplay networks trained with PPO for 100 million steps.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>While we don’t know if we’ll ever be able to download skills directly into our brains (<a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fhrNgXJ__n8">“I know kung fu”</a> - or for those in my particular demographic: <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_90">Joe 90</a>), this does raise the possibility of downloading capabilities into models by manipulating their weights.</p>

<p>Simon Wilison riffed on this paper by <a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Nov/3/qwen-can-interpret-model-weights/">examining whether an LLM can understand model behaviors from the weights alone</a>, by examining four simple neural networks, each trained to play a specific game, and figuring out which game it was by just looking at the weights.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/jessitron/status/2117400669044793405">Jessica Kerr (jessitron):</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>My days of teaching AI to do stuff are numbered.</p>

  <p>Getting it to teach me stuff is more fun.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Houck et al (that “al” includes some folks who I’ve learned to pay a lot of attention to), look at how we can assess the quality of context supplied to agents.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>An agent may draw from source code, specifications, issue trackers, chat histories, architectural documents, tool output, and user instructions, then act on that combination with limited human supervision. Information that was written for different audiences, at different times, and for different purposes is brought together into a temporary operating environment for a particular task. Whether each source is individually useful does not reveal whether the resulting context is fit for the work the agent is being asked to do.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>They’ve come up with a <a href="https://queue.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3847288">framework for describing the quality of this assembled context</a>, which they refer to as CAFE(S). The acronym refers to five dimensions they look at:</p>

<ul>
  <li>Clarity: Can the agent interpret the request as intended?</li>
  <li>Actionability: Can the agent proceed, and know when it is done?</li>
  <li>Fidelity: Can the agent trust that this context is true?</li>
  <li>Efficiency: Can the agent focus on what matters?</li>
  <li>Security: Should the agent have this context at all?</li>
</ul>

<p>They also list a set of half-a-dozen “context smells”: common cases where these break down (and a nod to Kent’s <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/CodeSmell.html">original metaphor</a>)</p>

<p>I’m not sure about the name, (how exactly should I pronounce “CAFE(S)”?) and the article exhibits rather more <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/LLM-Voice.html">LLM Voice</a> that I prefer. But on the whole I think this is a good framing for thinking about an important part of feeding our silicon servants/overlords.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Cindy Sridharan noted that <a href="https://x.com/copyconstruct/status/2118529738532925725">the problems she ran into six months ago are no longer around</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>What was missing in my setup 6 months ago was the verification process I now use.</p>

  <p>70% of my daily token spend goes into verifying patches, and only ~20-30% into creating them.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>That resonated with conversations I’ve had with folks using agents in enterprise settings. They are using far more tokens to check the working of models (through guardrails and evals) than they are to generate a response to a request. A single request to a model from a customer may trigger a dozen turns to other models to probe its input and verify its output.</p>

### [Long-horizon agents and durable execution](https://martinfowler.com/articles/durable-execution-agents.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/durable-execution-agents.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/durable-execution-agents/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>Long-horizon agents can automate complex tasks that take days or weeks
     to complete and involve multiple exchanges with people and external
     systems. Many things can go wrong during this time, including process
     crashes, network failures, and unanticipated real-world behavior.
     <b class="author">Kiran Prakash</b> explains durable execution: an emerging
     pattern that helps agents recover from these failures and move towards
     their goal. In this programming model, the runtime keeps a record of the
     result of each step that interacts with the outside world so that, when a
     failure occurs, the program runs again, completed steps are skipped, and
     the program's state is rebuilt from the record.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/durable-execution-agents.html">more…</a></p>

---

### [The Data Product Platform Navigator](https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>Many organizations adopting data mesh and data products face a common
     challenge. They may be tasked with building a self-service platform for
     data products and data mesh from scratch, or they may have an existing
     platform that suffers from low adoption, preventing their data mesh from
     scaling. This article provides a platform navigator, based on the CNCF's
     Platform Engineering Maturity Model, to help you reason about your current
     situation and determine the next steps for your platform.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav.html">more…</a></p>
